# Fact-checking & attribution — Star Stuff

Star Stuff pieces braid real science with attributed ideas from named thinkers and from our own
community. That only works if the facts and the attributions are right. This file holds the
guidelines and the per-piece ledger. The behavior rules are mirrored in `CLAUDE.md` so every
working session follows them.

*These are living documents. Fact-checking here is ongoing, not finished. If you spot an error or a
misattribution, please open an issue at <https://github.com/Stimpunks/Star-Stuff/issues> or reach us
at <https://stimpunks.org> — and we'll fix it.*

## Guidelines

### Attribution

- **Primary sources only.** Trace every quotation to the book, essay, paper, or talk it came from,
  and cite author + title + year. Never attribute from memory.
- **Aggregators and summaries are leads, not citations.** Quote sites (Goodreads, BrainyQuote,
  QuoteFancy), Wikipedia, our own stimpunks.org glossary, and any AI/search *summary* can point you
  toward a source but cannot be the source. Confirm the wording and the author against the primary
  text before it ships.
  - *Why this rule exists:* "We are caught in an inescapable network of mutuality" was shipped in
    the Symbioses field guide attributed to **Baldwin**. It is **Martin Luther King Jr.**, *Letter
    from Birmingham Jail* (1963). The error came from trusting a secondary summary. Helen Edgar's
    fact-check caught it.
- **Quote vs paraphrase.** A direct quote is verbatim, in quotation marks, and verifiable. A
  paraphrase characterizes someone's idea in our words. Never present a paraphrase as a quote.
- **Coinages** get their originator, precisely:
  - shared-air space, shared-signal space → **Helen Edgar** (with Ryan Boren)
  - neuro-anarchy → **Katie Munday** & **David Gray-Hammond**
  - the Autistic Rhizome → **David Gray-Hammond** / **Helen Edgar** (building on Deleuze & Guattari)
  - monotropism → **Murray, Lesser & Lawson (2005)**
  - double empathy → **Damian Milton (2012)**
- **When unverified:** cut it, mark it clearly as paraphrase, or hold the piece. Never ship an
  unverified claim as fact.

### Scientific claims

- Begin from well-established science; prefer primary or authoritative sources. Flag figures as
  approximate ("~", "roughly"). Keep the house honesty: no naturalistic fallacy, and name fragility
  or contested interpretations. As `about.html` puts it: *we are noticing a rhyme, not collecting a
  proof.*
- **Beware the science that flatters the metaphor.** A finding that fits our politics gets less
  scrutiny, which is exactly when it needs more.
  - *Why this rule exists:* the **"wood wide web"** — forests wired together, elder trees feeding
    shaded seedlings — shipped as settled fact in three pieces, because it rhymes so well with
    mutual aid. Karst, Jones & Hoeksema (*Nature Ecology & Evolution* 7, 2023) find the network's
    prevalence and its benefit to seedlings insufficiently supported, and the elder-feeds-offspring
    claim unevidenced. Mycorrhizal trade between fungus and root is settled; the forest-wide story
    is not. Corrected 2026-08-03. Keep the kinship, drop the overclaim.
- **A number can be wrong by having gone stale.** Measurements get superseded, and the old value
  keeps circulating in museum pages, planetarium copy and popular astronomy long after the
  literature has moved. Check a figure against current primary work, not against how often you can
  find it repeated — and when you cite a source for a number, confirm that source actually gives
  *that* number.
  - *Why this rule exists:* Zine No. 9 built its central spread on Orion's Belt lying at wildly
    different depths — Alnitak ~740 ly, Mintaka ~690, Alnilam ~2,000, "nearly three times farther."
    Those are Hipparcos-era parallaxes, and Hipparcos measured bright, distant, multiple O and B
    stars badly. Current values (VLTI interferometry, Gaia DR3, cluster membership) put all three
    within ~1,240–1,260 ly, all in the Orion OB1b association. Two things should have caught it
    earlier: the colophon cited APOD, which gives the three as a group at ~1,500 ly and never
    supported our figures; and **Field Guide No. 1 already said, correctly, that the belt stars are
    "a cohort" born in the same cloud** — the collection was contradicting itself in public.
    Corrected 2026-08-09. Cite the source you actually read, and read your own siblings.

### Status vocabulary

| Status | Meaning |
|--------|---------|
| `VERIFIED` | Confirmed against a primary/authoritative source. |
| `PLAUSIBLE` | The idea is sound and consistent with the author's work, but exact wording or a specific citable source was not primary-confirmed in this pass. |
| `CORRECTED` | An error was found and fixed (note what). |
| `REMOVED` | Claim/quote cut because it couldn't be supported. |

### Process

- **Per piece, before shipping:** list every quotation, named attribution, and key scientific
  claim; verify each to a primary source; fix what's wrong; log it below.
- **Periodically, across the repo:** re-check pieces on a rolling basis. When one source is found
  wrong, re-check its siblings for the same error (shared quotes travel between pieces).

## Ledger

Date format YYYY-MM-DD. "Checked" = a full attribution + key-claim pass was done.

| 6 | Eternal Sunshine | 2026-08-03; re-checked 2026-08-11 | CORRECTED → VERIFIED (one open) | **2026-08-11 — the 2026-08-03 correction was itself an overcorrection, and this is now the collection's sharpest example of a debunk going wrong.** That pass searched all thirteen chapters of the *book*, found the one-liner absent, and concluded it was a compression nobody had said. **Sagan said it aloud.** In the television series he speaks the line — "We are star stuff. Harvesting starlight." — **confirmed against the footage by Ryan Boren**, who located the clip. So the real fault in the popular quotation is narrower and more specific than invention: circulation changed **starlight → sunlight**, ran two sentences into one, and cited the *book*, where it has never been. Our correction, having checked one medium and concluded about all of them, called a real spoken sentence an invention. Both the essay *Too Good to Check* and the *Six Ways* broadside carried the wrong version of this story and were rewritten to carry the double failure instead, since it demonstrates the page's own "a debunking can be a misconception" refusal better than any outside example could. **Open:** the specific episode is not pinned. The archive.org copy of the book is lending-restricted and the Cosmos subtitle item is dark, so an independent transcript could not be reached; the wording rests on Ryan's check of the footage, which is a primary source, but the episode number should be recorded before the line is cited again. Original 2026-08-03 pass follows. **Fixed a paraphrase dressed as a quote.** The colophon presented `"We are star stuff harvesting sunlight" (Cosmos, 1980)` as Sagan's sentence. The colophon now says the epigraph is ours, quotes the two real sentences with the chapter, and keeps the in-zine "after Carl Sagan, *Cosmos*." | 9 | The Lines We Drew | 2026-08-09 | CORRECTED → VERIFIED | First check. **Five corrections, one of them structural.** (1) **The central astronomy was wrong.** Spread 3 and the colophon said Orion's Belt is a line-of-sight accident — Alnitak ~740 ly, Mintaka ~690, Alnilam ~2,000, "nearly three times farther" — and the diagram drew that depth. Those are Hipparcos-era parallaxes, and Hipparcos measured these bright, distant, multiple O/B stars badly. Current values put all three at essentially one depth: Alnitak 1,260 ± 180 ly (387 ± 54 pc); Alnilam 1,250 ± 26 ly (384 ± 8 pc) from VLTI interferometry — **Oplištilová, Brož, Hummel, Harmanec & Barlow, "VLTI observations of the Orion Belt stars: I. ε Orionis," *A&A* 704, A204 (2025)**, read at the primary, which states the star sits at the *median distance of the Orion OB1b association*; Mintaka ~1,240 ly (381 ± 8 pc, Gaia DR3 component C, against a superseded Hipparcos 212 ± 30 pc). All three belong to Orion OB1b — siblings from one cloud. **The zine's own cited source never supported its numbers:** APOD (ap051013) gives the three as a group "about 1,500 light-years away." **Field Guide No. 1 — the piece No. 9 is the companion to — already said it correctly** ("part of the same young star-forming region… not a coincidence. They are a cohort"), so the collection had been contradicting itself. Rewritten so the depth argument runs on the *figure* rather than the belt: Bellatrix 250 ± 10 ly, Betelgeuse ~548 (± ~100), Saiph 650 ± 30, Rigel ~863, belt ~1,250 — the hunter stitched across ~1,000 ly. Diagram redrawn as a depth ladder; heading "There is no belt" → "There is no **hunter**"; meta and `og:description` updated. The superseded figures are now *in* the zine as its own example: we drew a confident line in depth and better instruments moved it. (2) **Galton did not coin *eugenics* in *Hereditary Genius* (1869)** — he coined it in *Inquiries into Human Faculty and Its Development* (1883), in a footnote to the introduction proposing it for "the science of improving stock" (confirmed at galton.org's scan of the primary). Sentence split so 1869 keeps only the ranking and the "law of deviation from the average." (3) **Pearson chronology corrected:** he built the standard deviation (1894) and the correlation coefficient in the *Mathematical Contributions to the Theory of Evolution* series, and became the first Galton Professor of Eugenics at UCL only in 1911 (held to 1933) — the page had him doing the mathematics *as* chair-holder. (4) **"Pathology paradigm" is Nick Walker's, not Chapman's.** The colophon read "founding what Chapman calls the *pathology paradigm*." Walker coined it (and *neurodiversity paradigm*) in "Throw Away the Master's Tools: Liberating Ourselves from the Pathology Paradigm" (written 2011; published 2012 in *Loud Hands: Autistic People, Speaking*; revised 2013). Chapman works with the terms. (5) **"Neurodiversity was coined by Judy Singer (1998)" replaced with the collective account** per **Botha, Chapman, Giwa Onaiwu, Kapp, Stannard Ashley & Walker, "The neurodiversity concept was developed collectively: An overdue correction on the origins of neurodiversity theory," *Autism* (2024)**: "neurological diversity" and "neurodiversity" reached print in 1997 and 1998 via journalist Harvey Blume, who attributed them to the online Autistic community. Cross-linked to Asasumasu's *neurodivergent* (2000), already logged at No. 3. Also: the statistical sense of "normal" dated to the **1870s**, not "mid-1800s" — Peirce (1873), Galton, Lexis (1877), with "normal distribution" settled in Pearson by 1897. **Verified, unchanged:** Gauss *Theoria Motus* (1809) and the Ceres recovery, Legendre and Laplace; Quetelet directing the Brussels observatory and *Essai de physique sociale* (1835) inventing *l'homme moyen* as ideal rather than merely typical; DSM (homosexuality removed from 1973; Asperger's added DSM-IV 1994, merged DSM-5 2013); Fricker, *Epistemic Injustice* (2007), testimonial and hermeneutical; Chapman, *Empire of Normality* (2023); Fergus Murray, "Weird vs. Normal" (weirdpride.day/normality — title and author confirmed at the page). Further-reading dates all confirmed (Stigler 1986; Porter 1986; Hacking 1990; Davis 1995; Chaney 2022; Rose 2016). Render, screen contrast (10.5–17.1:1) and print output re-verified after the rewrite. |

The primary is **Brian Eno**, *A Year with Swollen Appendices* (Faber, 1996), in a letter to Dave Stewart among the appendices: "it means 'the intelligence and intuition of a whole cultural scene'. The altered wording appears in **five places across the Stimpunks Knowledge System** — `site/stimpunks.org/glossary/scenius.md`, `site/stimpunks.org/pages/bricolage.md` (twice, in two *different* variants), `notes/Commonplace Book/Scenius.md`, `notes/Resources/Unsorted/2025/New glossary page- scenius.md` — and Eno's actual wording appears in none of them. Eno's own caveat that it would be foolish to pretend everyone's contribution is equal is kept in the prose, so scenius doesn't flatten into erasing the person who did the work. | 32 | The Skeleton That Skipped Bone | 2026-08-10 | VERIFIED | First check, done during the build. Written for a young person in our community whose special interest is sharks, and built as the deliberate counter-song to **Bone Song** (1). **Read in the primary:** Dean, M. N. & Summers, A. P. (2006), "Mineralized cartilage in the skeleton of chondrichthyan fishes," *Zoology* 109(2), 164–168 — source of tessellated cartilage, the tesserae overlying an uncalcified core, and the three calcification types (areolar, globular, prismatic) varying with age, species, body region and *feeding behaviour*. Brazeau, M. D., Giles, S., Dearden, R. P., Jerve, A., Ariunchimeg, Y., Zorig, E., Sansom, R., Guillerme, T. & Castiello, M. (2020), "Endochondral bone in an Early Devonian 'placoderm' from Mongolia," *Nature Ecology & Evolution* 4, 1477–1484 — *Minjinia turgenensis* preserves endochondral bone, supporting a phylogenetically deeper origin for it and the chondrichthyan condition as a **secondary loss**. This is the zine's hinge and it inverts the textbook reading: the shark skeleton is **derived, not primitive**, which also makes the technical sense of "primitive" false here and not merely loaded. Same shape of fact as No. 31's decarcinization, and cross-linked to it. Ostrander, G. K., Cheng, K. C., Wolf, J. C. & Wolfe, M. J. (2004), "Shark cartilage, cancer and the growing threat of pseudoscience," *Cancer Research* 64(23), 8485–8491 — **42 documented chondrichthyan tumours** across at least 21 species in nine families and seven orders (24 sharks, 16 skates/rays, 2 chimaeroids), spanning nervous, digestive, integumentary, excretory, haematopoietic, reproductive, **skeletal** and endocrine systems, at least 15 malignant by invasion. Loprinzi, C. L., Levitt, R., Barton, D. L., Sloan, J. A., Atherton, P. J., Smith, D. J. et al. (2005), "Evaluation of shark cartilage in patients with advanced cancer: A North Central Cancer Treatment Group trial," *Cancer* 104(1), 176–182 — **no difference in overall survival or quality of life**, adverse effects driving poor compliance. **Verified:** the chondrichthyan fossil record reaches the early Silurian (~440 Ma) on scales and teeth, with *Doliodus problematicus* (Early Devonian) the oldest largely intact shark — the zine says "400+ Myr" and "call it 440 million years, on scales and teeth at first" rather than claiming skeletons that old. Piezoelectric bone credited to **Iwao Yasuda** (1954–1957), consistent with No. 1. **Held honestly** (in the colophon): the secondary-loss reading is where current evidence points and is a live area, not a closed one — Brazeau et al. present it as revived and supported, not settled; "no true bone" is stated to mean no *endochondral* bone, since chondrichthyans mineralise extensively and some rays carry bone-like tissue in denticles and thorns, an active question; the *reasons* a lineage sheds bone are not established and none is claimed. **Deliberately not claimed:** we could not verify a primary-source figure for how many sharks the cartilage trade killed, so the zine puts **no number on it** and asserts only what is documented — that the premise was false and the product did not work. Spread 10's refusals name the naturalistic fallacy, the "sharks are better" inversion, "losing a trait is secretly superior," the misreading that medicine is the enemy, blame aimed at patients rather than sellers, and disabled-people-as-animal-metaphor. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 12 spreads, 142 text elements composited against real ancestor backgrounds, min 4.46:1 with zero AA failures (the 4.46 element clears the large-text threshold); 99 SVG `<text>` nodes, min 6.20:1, zero below 4.5. Under emulated print the body inverts, all 12 spreads un-hide, min text 14.82:1 and min SVG text 15.91:1, zero failures — the only zero-height elements are `.nav-brand` and `.page-counter`, which print is meant to hide. Mobile verified at 390px with no horizontal overflow. **Diagram error caught by eye that every automated check passed:** the cover motif's shark read as a **tuna**, then as a **marlin** — a symmetric tail, an undersized mid-body dorsal, no visible pectorals, and on the second pass a needle snout. On a zine built for a reader whose special interest is sharks, that is the one audience certain to notice. Redrawn around the three cues that carry the silhouette: a heterocercal tail with a long thin upper lobe and a deep notch, a tall raked first dorsal, and long swept pectorals clear of the belly. The motif also overlapped the "Zine No. 32" eyebrow on the first pass — the same collision the No. 31 cover comment warns about — and is now boxed to viewBox x 8–180, y 22–145 with the constraint recorded in the file. **Screen-contrast defect found and fixed:** the house SVG label fills `rgba(136,134,160,0.85)` (4.16:1) and `rgba(193,101,58,0.95)` (4.28:1) fail AA on screen; replaced here with `rgba(156,152,178,0.95)` (6.17:1) and `rgba(214,124,80,1)` (6.11:1). **Closed 2026-08-10 by a site-wide sweep — and the sweep found the estimate in this row was wrong.** Auditing every page properly (compositing each SVG `<text>` fill against its real ancestor backgrounds in headless Chrome) found **226 failing text nodes across 27 pages and 20 distinct fills**, not 126 across 14 with 2 fills. Two lessons recorded because both would have caused damage: (1) **The first probe produced false positives** — it could not see SVG shapes painted *behind* text, so `elements-field-guide.html` reported all 8 of its text nodes at 1.05:1. Those are element **symbols drawn on filled accent discs** — dark-on-light, entirely correct. Mass-"fixing" them would have erased every symbol on the page. The probe was rewritten to composite rect/circle/ellipse fills painted before the text, and the field guide dropped off the list. (2) **The replacement value chosen from two zines was wrong for the collection.** `rgba(156,152,178,0.95)` gives 6.17:1 on the standard card ground but only **4.19:1** on the tinted panel grounds some diagrams use. The sweep instead standardises on the canonical **`--sp-muted`** token, `rgba(196,181,212,0.95)` — **6.00:1 worst-case across every ground on the site** — and Nos. 32–34 were retrofitted to it so the whole collection matches. **Rule now applied everywhere:** SVG text uses a palette token at alpha ≥ 0.95; off-palette darks were mapped to their canonical equivalents (`136,134,160`/`136,127,156`/`138,134,160`/`99,96,122` → muted; `193,101,58` → `214,124,80`; `219,39,119`/`236,72,153` → pink; `124,58,237` → violet; `240,238,248` → white). Fills were rewritten only inside `<text>` tags and on `<g>` wrappers carrying `font-*` attributes, so no shape or stroke colour was touched. **Three genuine defects surfaced that were not contrast-of-a-palette-value at all, and each was worse than the thing we set out to fix:** in `the-dead-stars-still-reach-us-zine.html` the label **"you" was near-white on the near-white figure it names — 1.36:1, invisible**; in `no-universal-now-zine.html` "room to move" sat on its own violet disc at 3.1:1; in `bone-song-zine.html` "forge" was gold on a gold shape at 2.47:1. All three now use dark text on the bright shape, the pattern `elements-field-guide.html` already had right. **And one was a layout bug, not a colour bug:** in `the-universe-runs-on-difference-zine.html` the label **"focus" was anchored `start` at x=18 and ran straight through its own green node** (circle cx=40 r=14, spanning x 26–54) at **1.21:1**. Recolouring would have hidden the real fault; it is now anchored `end` at x=22, clear of the circle and mirroring the "care" label opposite. Verified by eye at render size. **Result: 0 failing SVG text nodes across all 53 indexed pages**, screen and print, confirmed by re-running the audit. `search-index.json` was checked and is unaffected — no page text changed. |
| 33 | The Ick Is a Border | 2026-08-10 | CORRECTED | **Corrected the day it shipped, after review by Helen Edgar — see the correction note below the build-check notes.** First check, done during the build. House zine, and the deliberate companion to **No. 28 (Wyrd & Weird, Helen Edgar)** — *weird* is hers and reclaimed; this takes *gross*, which is disarmed rather than worn, and the distinction is made explicitly on spread 9 so the two pieces don't collide. **Read in the primary:** Smith, C. R. & Baco, A. R. (2003), "Ecology of whale falls at the deep-sea floor," *Oceanography and Marine Biology: An Annual Review* 41, 311–354 — the PDF was fetched and read rather than summarised, **which corrected a number**: secondary summaries gave ~100 macrofaunal species at the sulphophilic stage; the paper says a **mean of 185**, higher than any other deep-sea hard substratum community, with **407 species** globally on whale falls and **21 known only** from them. Also from the primary: the three stages and their durations (mobile-scavenger months to years; enrichment-opportunist months to years; sulphophilic **decades**), soft-tissue removal at **40–60 kg/day** by *sleeper sharks, hagfish and rat-tails*, opportunist densities up to **40,000 m⁻²**, and the initial pulse to underlying sediments equivalent to **~2,000 yr** of background organic-carbon flux. (The stage-one scavengers being sleeper sharks braids this zine to No. 32 in the source itself.) Swann, J. B., Holland, S. J., Petersen, M., Pietsch, T. W. & **Boehm, T.** (2020), "The immunogenetics of sexual parasitism," *Science* 369(6511), 1608–1615 — abstract read verbatim; author list confirmed against PubMed because early secondary sources listed only the first four. Temporarily-attaching species lack functional *aicda*; permanent attachment culminates in **loss of functional *rag* genes in some species, abolishing somatic diversification of antigen receptor genes, "the hallmark of canonical adaptive immunity."** Rouse, G. W., Goffredi, S. K. & Vrijenhoek, R. C. (2004), "Osedax: bone-eating marine worms with dwarf males," *Science* 305(5684), 668–671 — females consuming whale bone via a vascularised root system, lacking a discrete trophosome, with numerous dwarf paedomorphic males in the females' tubes. **Verified:** Rozin, P. & Fallon, A. E. (1987), "A perspective on disgust," *Psychological Review* 94(1), 23–41 — disgust as a food-related emotion (revulsion at oral incorporation), objects virtually all of animal origin, ontogeny across roughly the first eight years, and the laws of sympathetic magic including contagion. Nussbaum, M. C. (2004), *Hiding from Humanity: Disgust, Shame, and the Law* (Princeton) — *projective disgust*, and subordinated groups as buffers against the animal nature we would rather deny. **Attribution trap caught before it shipped:** "dirt is matter out of place" is routinely credited to **Mary Douglas** as her coinage. It is not. It appears unreferenced in the 1966 first edition of *Purity and Danger*, is credited in later editions to Lord Chesterfield without a source, and the earliest located instance is an 1853 address by **Lord Palmerston**. The zine does **not** use the line as hers and the colophon records the whole problem so the next piece to reach for it doesn't repeat the error. **Held honestly** (in the colophon): Rozin & Fallon is a theory of disgust, not the only one, and only the modest claim their evidence supports is made — that the *objects* are substantially learned and the response overshoots any physical fact; that some disgust tracks real pathogen risk is not disputed, and spread 10 refuses "disgust is never useful." The whale-fall figures are means from a review of a small, poorly sampled set of sites, which Smith and Baco say themselves, and are flagged as order-of-magnitude. The anglerfish immune losses vary by species and are not uniform; "gave up the line between self and other" is named as **our phrasing** of a genetic finding, with the note that adaptive immunity is not the whole of self/non-self discrimination. Spread 10's refusals name the naturalistic fallacy, "actually it's beautiful" (relocating rather than dropping the verdict), "gross but useful to humans" (the extraction frame No. 32 is about), blaming people for a reflex installed before consent, and disabled-people-as-deep-sea-metaphor. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 12 spreads, 145 text elements composited against real ancestor backgrounds, min 4.70:1 with zero AA failures; 127 SVG `<text>` nodes, min 6.20:1, zero below 4.5. Under emulated print the body inverts, all 12 spreads un-hide, min text 15.05:1 and min SVG text 15.91:1, zero failures. Mobile verified at 390px with no horizontal overflow. Same screen-contrast fix as No. 32; see its row. **CORRECTION (same day, post-publication). The zine over-claimed that disgust is learned, and the over-claim was caught by <strong>Helen Edgar</strong>**, who taught children with profound and multiple learning disabilities for twenty years. Her objection: the piece presumes a cognitive, intellectualising response to the ick, and pre-term babies and the young people she has worked with plainly have an ick without one — some of her PMLD students developed that newborn-type reflex response only at 3, 4, 5 or 6 years old, but it did come. She is right, and **Rozin's own framework already makes the distinction the zine collapsed.** *Distaste* is chemosensory rejection tied to a thing's real properties, producing the highly conserved "gape face" (gape, upper-lip retraction, nose wrinkle, brow furrow), present at birth and observed in utero. *Disgust* proper is, in Rozin's phrase, ideationally based and contamination-sensitive — you can be disgusted by a cockroach without tasting one. The decisive evidence for how deep distaste runs is **Steiner, J. E. (1973), "The gustofacial response: observation on normal and anencephalic newborn infants"** — anencephalic newborns produce it without most of a higher brain, so **the response does not require a cortex and therefore cannot be an opinion.** **Fixed by:** a new spread 3 ("Two icks, not one") placed *before* the argument, making the distinction and scoping everything downstream to the ideational kind; retitling the old spread 3 from "You weren't born disgusted" to "The second one gets taught"; two new refusals — that Autistic and otherwise neurodivergent **sensory aversion is distaste, not prejudice**, and that we are **not claiming a blank slate**; and rewriting the closing spread's "the ick was never in the water" to "the *verdict* was never in the water," with an added paragraph that not wanting to touch something is not the same as ruling it out of the world. The zine went from 12 spreads to 13. **Why this mattered beyond accuracy:** as first published, a reader with sensory processing differences could reasonably have read the zine as telling them their aversions were learned prejudice to be got over. Aimed at our own community, that would have done harm. **Helen's deeper point is now in the zine's voice and is the better framing:** *we are not blank slates — that is the point of star stuff; we have history in us.* A response conserved across species and present before birth **is** that history, and the original draft was arguing against this collection's own founding premise without noticing. Credited to her on the spread and in the colophon. |

(2012), "The slowest fish: swim speed and tail-beat frequency of Greenland sharks," *J. Exp. Mar. Biol. Ecol.* 426–427, 5–11 — six free-swimming sharks, cruising **0.34 m s⁻¹**, tail-beat **0.15 Hz**, both the lowest for their size across fish species, plus the sleeping-seal proposal. Borucinska, J. D., Benz, G. W. & Whiteley, H. E. (1998), *J. Fish Dis.* 21(6), 415–422 — corneal lesions from *Ommatokoita elongata*, the bulla and its associated opacity. **Verified:** growth <1 cm/yr; depths recorded to ~2,647 m; water to about −1.8 °C; IUCN Red List **Vulnerable**. **BGUTI** is **Alfie Kohn**'s, from "Getting Hit on the Head Lessons," *Education Week*, **7 September 2005** — article fetched and read; both quotations on spread 9 are verbatim from it ("Almost by definition, the BGUTI defense ignores developmental differences" and "People don't really get better at coping with unhappiness because they were deliberately made unhappy when they were young"). The blunter formulation — we don't prepare students for a world of potential oppression by oppressing them — is **Stimpunks' own**, from stimpunks.org, and is marked as ours rather than as Kohn's. Slow pedagogy is Stimpunks' glossary entry, which follows **Alison Clark**, *Slow Knowledge and the Unhurried Child*; credited to Clark rather than to us. Social model of disability credited to the British disabled people's movement — UPIAS, *Fundamental Principles of Disability* (1976) — and **Mike Oliver**, who named it. **Held honestly** (in the colophon): the Nielsen figures carry real uncertainty and are stated as such — 272 is a *minimum*, 392 carries ±120, and age at maturity is "at least" 156 ± 22 and is **inferred from body length, not observed**. Borucinska et al. examined **six** sharks and found the copepod on all six; very high prevalence is widely reported but rests on small samples, so **no population percentage is stated in the prose** — an earlier draft would have carried a secondary-sourced "99%" and it was cut. That the sharks don't *need* their eyes is flagged as a well-supported **inference** from habitat and other senses, not a measured finding. The recovery curves on spread 7 are labelled **on the figure itself** as "schematic · not measured data" — they illustrate a mechanism, they are not plotted from a population model. **The naturalistic-fallacy risk was the main editorial problem here** and is handled structurally rather than with a closing disclaimer: spread 7 is the conservation alarm and sits *before* any application to people, so the cost of slowness is established before its consolations. Spread 10's refusals name "slow is better," "you're secretly a Greenland shark," "everyone just needs more time," "impairment is imaginary," "a reason to skip support" (the social model must not become an excuse to withhold help while waiting for redesign), the "but the real world won't wait" BGUTI rejoinder, and the fallacy itself. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 13 spreads, 164 text elements composited against real ancestor backgrounds, min 4.46:1 with zero AA failures (that element clears the large-text threshold); 141 SVG `<text>` nodes, min 5.73:1, zero below 4.5 — the corrected label fills from No. 32 were used from the start. Under emulated print the body inverts, all 13 spreads un-hide, min text 14.82:1 and min SVG text 15.91:1, zero failures. Mobile verified at 390px, no horizontal overflow. Cover motif checked at render size and drawn deliberately *unlike* No. 32's: *Somniosus microcephalus* has tiny dorsal fins, small pectorals, a blunt snout and a short-lobed tail, so reusing No. 32's tall raked dorsal would have been both wrong and a lie about the animal. |
| 35 | Everywhere That Turtles Go | 2026-08-10 | VERIFIED | First check, done during the build. House zine, 13 spreads. Frame is **Tyson Yunkaporta** (Apalech clan, Wik), *Sand Talk: How Indigenous Thinking Can Save the World* (2019) — quoted from **p. 15** (the metastory, the Star Dreaming, and "It goes everywhere that turtles go—and there are turtles all over the world, even in desert country, so it connects everybody") and **pp. 225–226** (Oldman Juma's sequence). Both passages are also in Ryan's Readwise (Locations 196 and ~3200). **Protocol decision, taken before writing:** the Star Dreaming is **Oldman Juma Fejo**'s, of the **Larrakia People**, and Yunkaporta records in the book that it was given to be shared with all peoples. We therefore **quote the published account and never retell the story in our own voice** — it is not ours to narrate. This is stated in the colophon. **Read in the primary:** Lyson, T. R., Rubidge, B. S., Scheyer, T. M., de Queiroz, K., Schachner, E. R., Smith, R. M. H., Botha-Brink, J. & Bever, G. S. (2016), "Fossorial origin of the turtle shell," *Current Biology* 26(14), 1887–1894 — rib broadening as an adaptive response to fossoriality in *Eunotosaurus africanus*; broadened ribs **"provide little protection"** while imposing locomotory and respiratory costs; the current protective function is an **exaptation**. Lyson, T. R., Schachner, E. R., Botha-Brink, J., Scheyer, T. M., Lambertz, M., Bever, G. S., Rubidge, B. S. & de Queiroz, K. (2014), "Origin of the unique ventilatory apparatus of turtles," *Nature Communications* 5, 5211 — rigid ribcage, inferred loss of intercostal muscles, and ventilation taken over by an **abdominal muscular sling**, a division of labour between ribs and trunk muscles. Brothers, J. R. & Lohmann, K. J. (2015), "Evidence for geomagnetic imprinting and magnetic navigation in the natal homing of sea turtles," *Current Biology* 25(3), 392–396 — **19 years** of nesting data along **600 km** of Florida coast, nesting density tracking drift in the field. da Silva, R., Conde, D. A., Baudisch, A. & Colchero, F. (2022), "Slow and negligible senescence among testudines…," *Science* 376, 1466–1470 — **~75% of 52 species** slow or negligible; ~80% ageing more slowly than modern humans. **Corrected before it shipped — the "shoulder blade inside the ribcage" claim.** The popular statement (and several secondary sources found during the check) is that the turtle scapula sits *inside the ribcage*, unique among tetrapods. **Lyson, T. R. & Joyce, W. G. (2012), "Evolution of the turtle bauplan: the topological relationship of the scapula relative to the ribcage," *Biology Letters* 8(6), 1028–1031** finds that "the scapula remains **anterior** to the first dorsal rib even in adult turtles," and that a vertical scapula anterior to the thoracic ribcage is the basal amniote condition — the scapula is enclosed by the **shell** (which includes dermal bone beyond the ribcage), not by the ribcage. **A draft spread asserted the looser version; it was cut rather than hedged**, and the colophon records why. **Second conflation caught: Kurma is not a world-bearer.** In the Puranic literature Vishnu's tortoise form is a **pivot on the seabed for Mount Mandara during the churning of the ocean**. The "world resting on a turtle's back" story is not this story, and the merge is a Western habit; the zine states the distinction on spread 11 and on the figure. **Third: "turtles all the way down" is not William James, Bertrand Russell or Hawking's little old lady.** James's 1882 *Princeton Review* essay uses **rocks**, not turtles; the turtle punchline was attached to him in John Robert Ross's 1967 doctoral thesis; the earliest located printed instance is 1854. Recorded on spread 11 rather than used. **Verified:** ~**357** recognised modern species with **171** (47.9%) assessed as globally threatened — Turtle Taxonomy Working Group / IUCN Tortoise and Freshwater Turtle Specialist Group, *Turtles of the World: Annotated Checklist and Atlas* (2021 ed.; a 2025 10th ed. raises the count to ~364, and the zine says "roughly"). Turtles on every continent but Antarctica. Arid-zone aestivation in ***Chelodina steindachneri*** (Pilbara/Gascoyne, WA) — buries in riverbed or bank through dry periods, surfacing after heavy rain; Legler & Georges, *Fauna of Australia* Vol. 2A. *Gopherus agassizii* spending **up to 95%** of life underground, and its burrows sheltering other species (**keystone species**) — USFWS and the Desert Tortoise Council. **Pleurodira** restricted to fresh water in former Gondwanan landmasses (South America, Africa–Madagascar, Australia) while **Cryptodira** are cosmopolitan and are the only turtles in the oceans. Temperature-dependent sex determination and its machinery — Ge, C. et al. (2018), *Science* 360, 645–648 (KDM6B removing H3K27me3 near the *Dmrt1* promoter); Weber, C. et al. (2020), *Science* 368, 303–306 (pSTAT3 repression of *Kdm6b* at the female-producing temperature); and the **coexistence of XY chromosomes with TSD** in *Mauremys mutica*, *Molecular Ecology* (2024) — included specifically so the spread does not present a clean genetic-vs-environmental binary. Scute plan of a typical carapace: one nuchal, **five vertebrals down the midline**, four pairs of pleurals, ~12 pairs of marginals, with scutes not corresponding to the bones beneath. Piezoelectric bone is **Iwao Yasuda**'s (1950s) — see No. 1. **Held honestly** (spread 10 and the colophon): **the Fibonacci passage is carried and its overreach is named on the page rather than quietly omitted.** Yunkaporta reports Oldman Juma's sequence (1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13) and then extends it to the golden ratio "that all nature is built upon, from flowers and trees to your body and even DNA." The **five vertebral scutes down the midline are real and countable**, and the zine says so. The general golden-ratio-in-nature claim, and specifically the golden ratio in DNA, are **not supported**, and the zine says that too. The judgement made here: carrying a knowledge system faithfully includes not laundering an empirical claim through it, and the count in the shell is not made better by a modern number nor worse by that number being over-claimed. Likewise the "expansion and contraction" cosmology in the source is **not** presented as physics; the honest resonance offered instead is *breathing*, against the real finding that turtles had to replace rib ventilation. **The songline/natal-homing resemblance is named as a rhyme on the page itself** — "The Larrakia story is not turtle magnetoreception, and turtle magnetoreception is not a songline. Two true things, next to each other." ***Odontochelys semitestacea*** (Li et al., *Nature* 456, 2008 — plastron complete, carapace not) was researched and **deliberately not made load-bearing**, because the plastron-first reading is contested against secondary reduction. **Shipped `PLAUSIBLE`, closed to `VERIFIED` the same day.** The Didactylos passage was drafted from aggregators (Goodreads, Reddit), so the zine shipped using only a fragment plus the three-word slogan, with the colophon stating the wording was unconfirmed. **Ryan then checked it against the book and sent the page.** It is verbatim: "I just wrote down what people know. Mountains rise and fall, and under them the Turtle swims onward. Men live and die, and the Turtle Moves. Empires grow and crumble, and the Turtle Moves. Gods come and go, and still the Turtle Moves. The Turtle *Moves*." — **Terry Pratchett**, *Small Gods* (1992), Didactylos addressing the crowd, with the italic falling on the final *Moves*. The page also confirms the surrounding exchange the aggregators ran together as one block: the enquirer ("Sir, surely only things that exist are worth believing in?") is a sergeant of the Holy Guard, and Didactylos's "The Turtle *exists*… And this will go on happening, whether you believe it is true or not. **It is real.** I don't know about truth. Truth is a lot more complicated than that." is a **separate speech**, delivered after an interruption from the darkness. **Because of that interruption the two runs are not spliced**, even with an ellipsis — the zine quotes the first run whole as a pull-quote and characterises the second in our own words, quoting only "It is real." A splice would have elided another speaker's turn, which is the same class of error as dressing a paraphrase as a quote. The colophon keeps the note about having shipped it unconfirmed rather than deleting it now that it checks out. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 455 text elements composited against real ancestor backgrounds across all 13 spreads, **min 4.70:1, zero AA failures**; under emulated print the body inverts, all 13 spreads un-hide, **min 13.92:1, zero failures** — the page aliases the `--sp-*` tokens rather than hardcoding hexes, which is what earns the print inversion. **Five diagram faults caught at render size, none of which any automated check would have found:** the cover motif's desert tortoise sat on the "35" corner numeral and its "RANGE" label; an "every warm ocean" caption ran through the cover title once the motif was shifted clear (cut — the waves and the sea turtle already say ocean); the burrow, drawn as a closed fill path, came out a trapezoid with the turtle below its floor (redrawn as one thick translucent stroke so the void has a constant bore); **the cryptodire's neck looped outside the shell, reading as a neck extended — the exact opposite of the caption**, so both turtles were redrawn with the head inside the outline; and "↯" is not in Space Mono and fell back to a glyph rendering as a **4**, so the charge symbol is now drawn as a path. Each fix carries a comment in the source saying what went wrong. |

It also names the reply as itself an argued position by invested researchers. The neurodiversity answer is framed as **an absence we searched for, not a proven absence** (English-language search, not exhaustive), and the piece explicitly refuses "whales are smarter than us," cross-species diagnosis, the superpower framing, and the naturalistic fallacy — including a final refusal naming its own extraction of cetacean minds for a human argument. |
| 37 | Out of Order | 2026-08-14 | CORRECTED → VERIFIED (four open items) | First check, done during the build. House zine, 9 spreads, filed in the new *Easter Eggs* collection; the number fills the single deliberate gap in the sequence and the piece marks itself `leap` for doing it. **The commissioning premise was itself wrong, and correcting it became the zine's opening move.** The brief was "we accidentally skipped No. 37, so here is an apology to the number." `DECISIONS.md` says the opposite in plain words: 37 was **assigned to *The Cloud Phase* on 2026-08-11, deliberately held pending a question to Helen Edgar rather than built on an assumption, and shipped 2026-08-13 as No. 45** because seven pieces had taken the intervening numbers — "the number says *when*, and that gap is the rule working, not a renumbering," quoted verbatim on spread 2. Shipping "we lost track of one" would have been the smoothed retelling `too-good-to-check.html` catalogues, committed by us, in a piece about honesty, for a joke. **Read at or near primary:** Protsiv, M., Ley, C., Lankester, J., Hastie, T. & Parsonnet, J. (2020), "Decreasing human body temperature in the United States since the Industrial Revolution," *eLife* 9:e49555, doi:10.7554/eLife.49555 — article read directly; source of the monotonic decline of **0.03 °C per birth decade**, **−0.59 °C in men born 1800–1997**, **−0.32 °C in women born 1890–1997**, the modern mean nearer **36.6 °C**, and Wunderlich's figure of "37 °C or 98.6 °F (range: 36.2–37.5 °C)". **Verified by hand rather than cited, which is the point of the spread:** 37 is the 12th prime and 73 the 21st, each the other's digit reversal and both prime; star numbers 6*n*(*n*−1)+1 give 1, 13, 37, 73, so 37 is the third and 73 the fourth; centered hexagonal numbers 3*n*(*n*−1)+1 give 1, 7, 19, 37, so 37 is the fourth; 111 = 3 × 37, which generates the repdigits. Every one of these was checked on paper. **Held `contested` on its face:** the claim that 37 is the most-chosen "random" number from 1 to 100, from a survey of roughly 200,000 responses run by **Veritasium** — a large self-published experiment, not a refereed finding, and every account of it we could reach is secondary, so spread 7 carries a `contested` joint tag saying so and presents it as *somebody's experiment* rather than as a fact about people. **The refusal is the spine:** spread 5 exists to refuse the connection the piece most wants to make, since the popular telling solders 1/e ≈ 37% onto "people pick 37" as though one caused the other. The three 37s have different units and are different kinds of object (a 19th-century arithmetic mean in °C; a dimensionless limit; a base-ten habit over a 1–100 range), and 37 °C is 98.6 in Fahrenheit, where the coincidence evaporates. Named as the same fault as No. 40's scientism — a coincidence dressed as a mechanism. **Stated as a cost, not smoothed:** the colophon says outright that filling a deliberate gap destroys evidence, that the original record survives only in `DECISIONS.md` and the changelog, and that it must not happen twice. **Open (all four are "primary not read," each flagged in the colophon rather than left implicit):** (1) Wunderlich, C. A., *Das Verhalten der Eigenwärme in Krankheiten* (Leipzig: Otto Wigand, 1868; English trans. New Sydenham Society, 1871) — the 1868 imprint, the ~25,000 patients, the million-plus axillary readings, the foot-long thermometer and the ~20-minute reading are from library, bookseller and secondary medical-history records, not from the book; (2) **a date discrepancy we are deliberately not smoothing** — Protsiv et al. date Wunderlich's establishment of the figure to **1851** while the book carrying it is **1868**; the zine cites the book by its own date and flags the difference in the colophon rather than silently picking one; (3) Bruss, F. T. (1984), "A unified approach to a class of best choice problems with an unknown number of options," *Annals of Probability*, for the 1/e law, plus Flood (1949) and Gardner's *Scientific American* column of February 1960 — the rule and its 1/e success probability are from standard reference treatments and phrased to match what those say was proved; Ferguson, T. S. (1989), "Who solved the secretary problem?", *Statistical Science* 4, is cited as the account of the attribution rather than of the theorem; (4) *g*(5) = 37 in Waring's problem, attributed to **Chen Jingrun, 1964** — from standard histories of the problem, stated no more strongly than they do. Screen and print contrast measured (372 elements, 76 SVG labels) after one real failure was found and fixed: `--ember` measures 4.78:1 on bare `--page` but only **4.43:1** once a tinted callout lifts the ground under it, so the joint tags now carry *How We Got Here*'s own notation colours (gold for contested, pink for leap) instead. Markup, sitemap, sheet and search-index gates clean. |

Thorndike's *Animal Intelligence* (1898) completed under **James McKeen Cattell** at Columbia (supervision confirmed, so that joint is `documented`); Cattell's "mental tests" (1890); Wundt's Leipzig laboratory (1879). The credo — "Whatever exists at all exists in some amount. To know it thoroughly involves knowing its quantity as well as its quality" — in "The Nature, Purposes, and General Methods of Measurements of Educational Products," *The Measurement of Educational Products*, **Seventeenth Yearbook of the NSSE**, Part II (1918). Frederick J. Kelly, *Teachers' Marks, Their Variability and Standardization* (Teachers College Contributions to Education no. 66, 1914) and the **Kansas Silent Reading Test** as the first published multiple-choice test. Pressey's 1924 device built from typewriter parts to administer and score multiple-choice items; Skinner, "The Science of Learning and the Art of Teaching," *Harvard Educational Review* (1954); Watters, *Teaching Machines* (MIT Press, 2021). Chomsky, "The Case Against B. F. Skinner," *NYRB*, 30 December 1971 ("quite vacuous"; "as congenial to the libertarian as to the fascist"). Lovaas, *J. Consult. Clin. Psychol.* **55**(1), 1987, pp. 3–9 — 47% of the experimental group (n=19) against 2% of controls (n=40), with "normal functioning" operationalized as normal-range IQ and successful first grade. **Two corrections to the source essay**, [The Road to Neuronormative Domination](https://stimpunks.org/2024/05/10/the-road-to-neuronormative-domination-thorndike-won-dewey-lost-skinner-won-papert-lost/), both carried into the zine's colophon: (1) **Cubberley's factory sentence is *Public School Administration* (1916), p. 338** — "our schools are, in a sense, factories, in which the raw products (children) are to be shaped and fashioned into products to meet the various demands of life" — not a 1905 dissertation as the essay gives it; (2) **Thorndike did not develop multiple-choice testing.** Kelly did, in 1914. Thorndike's contribution is the measurement credo and the apparatus. **Attribution checked and stated carefully:** Lagemann's line is quoted as she wrote it, hedged — "I have often argued to students, only in part to be perverse, that one cannot understand the history of education in the United States during the twentieth century unless one realizes that Edward L. Thorndike won and John Dewey lost" — *History of Education Quarterly* **29**(2), 1989, pp. 185–214; the zine passes it on as the provocation she framed it as rather than as a verdict. "Seymour Papert lost and B. F. Skinner won" is **Audrey Watters**, "B. F. Skinner: The Most Important Theorist of the 21st Century," *Hack Education*, 18 October 2018, quoted verbatim; **Ryan Boren's "Skinner won, Papert lost" is his own reformulation after Watters**, and is credited as such. *Pathology paradigm* attributed to **Nick Walker**, not Chapman — the error corrected at No. 9 was specifically guarded against here, since the source essay's framing invites it. Galton → Kraepelin → DSM is not re-derived; it is cited to No. 9, where it is already primary-confirmed. **Open:** the contested first joint rests on **Matthew D. Lund, "'A Vitious Way of Observing': Kinnebrook and the Prehistory of the Personal Equation," *Isis* **116**(3), 2025** — publisher, PhilArchive and Rowan Digital Works all returned HTTP 403, so **the paper has not been read at the primary**. A historical perspective on the transmission of respiratory infectious diseases," *Interface Focus* **11**(6), 20210049 (2021), DOI 10.1098/rsfs.2021.0049, accepted 13 Sept 2021, open access CC BY 4.0 — all eight pages read. **Confirmed verbatim in it:** the WHO's "FACT: COVID-19 is NOT airborne" and its "misinformation" framing; the CDC's March 2020 droplet statement and its not using "airborne"/"aerosol" until October 2020; the July 2020 letter from 239 scientists; the four tenets and "Yet, a relationship between these assertions is not supported by current scientific knowledge"; Wells' **100 µm** threshold, his diffusion-based evaporation model "in idealized quiescent conditions applied to isolated droplets" with settling from Stokes' speed, the note that indoor air is never quiescent, and his table 1 timings (5 µm ≈ 30 min from 1.5 m; 10 µm ≈ 10 min from 2 m; **1 µm ≈ 16 h** from 2 m); the statement that Wells' 1934 papers do not mention a 5 µm threshold; the Flügge team's plate distances (2 m speakers, 4 m coughers, 9 m sneezers), Koeniger's 12.5 m, the **House of Commons dispatch box experiment with *Bacillus prodigiosus* and Shakespeare, colonies past 21 m**, and the five-hour wait before plate collection; Flügge's use of "droplets" for all respiratory emissions wet or dry; Chapin (1856–1941), *The Sources and Modes of Infection* (1910), and the twenty years of short-range focus; "clearly a misrepresentation of the full body of Flügge's work"; the miasma-resemblance objection; industrial hygiene's 1–5 µm deep-lung finding; **Langmuir's 1951 biological-warfare lecture months after the start of the Korean War**, quoted exactly; his 1964 remarks; the SARS-CoV-2 tropism point (aerosols up to the 100 µm inhalable limit can initiate infection, unlike TB); the three-definitions citation trace and the Siegal-citing-Duguid example; "Despite the prominence of this size threshold in the literature, a 5 µm threshold … is not scientifically grounded"; the Crimean War 3-ft barracks recommendation, Ziesché & Flügge's ~1 m tied to an extrapolated 400-bacilli dose, the 1 m / 1.5 m / 2 m divergence, and the 1940s **photographic observation window of 1–2 m** that "reinforced the collective focus on this 1–2 m distance"; the word **puzzling** for the size-and-distance link; and the Cretyl Mills account — "the brunt of this tedious multi-year work was carried forward by Cretyl Mills, who subsequently contracted TB" — plus Riley's omission of Wells' name and his "eternal shame". **The centre of the zine is a hedge, quoted as a hedge:** "Though no association is made explicit in any of the literature we reviewed, this focus on particle deposition in the lungs **likely** popularized the 5 µm diameter cut-off." The joint is marked `contested` for exactly that reason; firming it up would commit the error the zine describes. **Open (1):** *Langmuir's 1980 retrospective* — the admission that Wells was right about measles, the "doctrinal" teaching remark, and "eternal skepticism of the true scientist" — is carried **via this paper's characterisation**, not read at source. **Open (2):** the *2024 WHO report removing the droplet/aerosol dichotomy* is likewise not read at source; we have the responding commentary in *Aerosol Science and Technology* (2024), including its own title judgement that the change "does not move us forward in infection control strategies." Both are stated as held-honestly in the zine's colophon rather than asserted. **Marked `leap` on its face:** the Stokes bridge to interstellar dust on spread 6 — the settling regime genuinely is continuous, and it licenses nothing about a classroom. **Credits:** testimonial injustice to Miranda Fricker, *Epistemic Injustice* (2007), already primary-confirmed at No. 9; deficit-versus-structural to Paul Gorski; fundamental attribution error to Lee Ross. **Mildred Wells and Cretyl Mills are named on a spread**, the source paper having listed both as "not pictured." House zine, 16 spreads, *How We Got Here* (third chain). **Spine read at the primary, all six pages:** Louis Lyons, "Discovering the Significance of 5σ," **arXiv:1310.1284v1, 4 October 2013**. Every quotation on spreads 2–7 is verbatim from it, checked against the PDF rather than a summary — and that mattered: **a WebFetch summary of this paper stated that Lyons "does not argue the threshold should vary by experiment," which is the exact opposite of his thesis.** His abstract asks "whether a more nuanced criterion might be better"; §3 is headed *"Why not 5σ?"* and opens "There are several reasons why it is not sensible to use a uniform criterion of 5σ for all searches for new physics." Had the summary been trusted, the zine's entire argument would have been inverted. **Confirmed verbatim:** the convention and its *p*-value ("the area beyond 5σ in one tail of a normalised Gaussian distribution, or smaller; i.e. the *p*-value is no larger than 3 × 10⁻⁷"); journals' reluctance to permit "discovery"; the History argument ("many 'phenomena' that corresponded to 3 or 4σ effects that have gone away when more data were collected"); "Statisticians who hear about the 5σ criterion are very skeptical about it being sensible" and "Thus the Statisticians' criticism may well be valid"; local vs global *p*-values and "It is clearly important to decide in advance of the analysis what procedure will be used"; the subconscious Bayes' factor with the energy-non-conservation example and "There is sense to the oft-quoted maxim 'Extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence'"; the systematics arithmetic ("a 5σ claim should be reduced to merely 2.5σ if the systematic uncertainties had been underestimated by a factor of 2 … the *p*-value increasing from 3 × 10⁻⁷ by a dramatic factor of 2 × 10⁴") and "It is far better to have a procedure which allows in some way for uncertain systematics than to raise the significance level for all experiments"; **Table 1** and its columns (Search / Degree of surprise / Impact / LEE / Systematics / Number of σ), from which single top 3, Higgs 5, direct dark matter 5, SUSY 7, gravitational waves 7 and v<sub>ν</sub> > c ">8" are quoted; and "The spirit of these numbers is to provoke discussion of this issue (rather than being a rigid set of rules), by suggesting a graded set of significance levels." **Fisher:** *Statistical Methods for Research Workers* (1925) — "The value for which P=0.05, or 1 in 20 is 1.96 or nearly 2; it is convenient to take this point as a limit in judging whether a deviation is to be considered significant or not." The **~170,000×** figure is our own arithmetic, 0.05 ÷ (3 × 10⁻⁷) ≈ 166,667, stated as approximate on the page because both thresholds are conventions. **Adverse events:** Bottema-Beutel, Crowley, Sandbank & Woynaroski, "Adverse event reporting in intervention research for young autistic children," *Autism* **25**(2), 2021, 322–335 — 150 reports reviewed, **11 mentioned adverse events**; of 54 reporting withdrawal reasons, 10 were categorisable as adverse events and 12 too vague to classify. A systematic review and meta-analysis with recommendations for best practices," *Psychological Bulletin* (2023; online first 3 Nov 2022) — 63 studies, N = 97,672; publication bias, authors with financial incentives more likely to report positive effects, major internal-validity threats, and **higher-quality studies less likely to show benefit**. **Credits:** *epistemic injustice* to Miranda Fricker (2007), already primary-confirmed at No. 9; its neurodivergence application to Robert Chapman, "Neurodiversity, epistemic injustice, and the good human life," *J. Social Philosophy* (2022); *measure the surface, badly* to the Stimpunks behaviorism glossary via [On the Problems with Science of Reading](https://stimpunks.org/2023/11/22/on-the-problems-with-science-of-reading/); Seidenberg on the SoR case resting on "a small number of concepts taken from a few simple, dated studies"; *pathology paradigm*, where implied, remains Nick Walker's. **Marked `contested` and argued against on its own spread:** applying Lyons' four dials to interventions on children is our extension, not his — spread 10 makes it, spread 11 gives the four strongest objections (existence vs effect-size claims, no neutral null, no identical trials, false negatives are harms too) at full strength rather than in a hedge. House zine, 15 spreads, *How We Got Here* (fourth chain). **Haraway read at the primary**, not from a summary or an anthology extract: Donna Haraway, "Situated Knowledges: The Science Question in Feminism and the Privilege of Partial Perspective," *Feminist Studies* **14**(3), Autumn 1988, 575–599, pages 580–585 read directly. Every quotation is verbatim with its page: the conquering gaze and "makes the unmarked category claim the power to see and not be seen, to represent while escaping representation" (581); "the god trick of seeing everything from nowhere" (581) and "that view of infinite vision is an illusion, a god trick" (582); **"The moral is simple: only partial perspective promises objective vision"** and "Feminist objectivity is about limited location and situated knowledge, not about transcendence and splitting of subject and object. It allows us to become answerable for what we learn how to see" (583); "Relativism is a way of being nowhere while claiming to be everywhere equally," "Relativism is the perfect mirror twin of totalization," "Relativism and totalization are both 'god tricks'," and the subjugated standpoints as "not 'innocent' positions" (584); "not just any partial perspective will do" (585). **Reading the primary changed the zine twice.** First, the received version of Haraway is that she attacks objectivity; the essay says the opposite in a sentence she flags as its moral, so spread 9 argues that *situatedness is the precondition of objectivity* rather than its refutation. Second, her refusal of relativism (584) is at least as sharp as her critique of the god trick — a guard rail that most secondary accounts drop entirely, and without which this zine would read as "everything is subjective." It is now a whole spread and a refusal. Also confirmed from the text: her astronomical imagery is hers, not our framing — "the brilliant space platforms of the powerful" (583) and the quoted epigraph "The stuff of stars has come alive" (582). **Parallax:** Bessel's 1838 parallax for 61 Cygni of **0.3136″**, measured at Königsberg with a Fraunhofer heliometer, giving ~10.4 ly against a modern ~11.4; Henderson (α Centauri) and Struve (Vega) within months. The "two-centimetre coin at thirteen kilometres" is **our own arithmetic** from that angle (0.3136″ ≈ 1.52 × 10⁻⁶ rad; 0.02 m ÷ that ≈ 13.2 km) and is labelled approximate. The pre-Bessel objection and the star-size problem are given qualitatively; the spurious discs are a finite-aperture diffraction artefact. **Leavitt:** joined Harvard College Observatory 1895 as one of the women "computers"; measured periods for roughly 1,700 variables across the Magellanic Clouds; the period–luminosity relation published as "Periods of 25 Variable Stars in the Small Magellanic Cloud," *Harvard College Observatory Circular* **173** (1912), under director Edward Pickering's name and opening that the statement **"has been prepared by Miss Leavitt."** **WEIRD:** Henrich, Heine & Norenzayan, "The weirdest people in the world?", *Behavioral and Brain Sciences* **33**(2–3), 2010, 61–83 — 96% of samples from Western industrialised nations, 68% of subjects from the US, from countries holding ~12% of world population, and WEIRD subjects as frequent outliers. **Double empathy** to Damian Milton, *Disability & Society* **27**(6), 2012, 883–887. **Not primary-sourced, and flagged as such on the page:** the remark that recalibrating the variable-star rung has historically moved the whole extragalactic distance scale is stated qualitatively without figures. **Two joints marked `contested` and argued in place:** the parallax-to-situated-knowledges parallel (spread 9 — Haraway never mentions parallax, the analogy is ours, and the spread says a theorem cannot settle an epistemology), and the personal-equation-to-research-practice synthesis (spread 13 — Bessel did not intend a research-ethics template, and the empirical claim that participatory methods yield better *findings* is flagged as needing its own evidence). **The three-erasures spread (7)** documents Cretyl Mills, Mildred Wells and Leavitt individually; reading them as a pattern is our interpretation, we have not searched for counter-examples, and the spread carries a note saying exactly that. **No `leap` joints** — 10 documented, 2 contested — which is now **two chains running leap-free**; the colophon flags this as something to watch rather than a badge, since a tag that never fires may reflect subject selection rather than rigour. Counts verified programmatically against the file rather than by hand after an off-by-one on No. 40. Screen and print contrast measured (548 elements, 117 SVG labels); markup, sitemap and chain gates clean. |

Nathan's five dangerous assumptions, Ben Williamson on SEL's backers, and Paul Gorski's equity-literacy sentence are carried from the Stimpunks essay and its citations rather than re-traced to each primary; marked open on that basis. **Three joints `contested` and argued in place:** the tower story; the physics-to-deficit-ideology transfer on spread 6, whose note states plainly that children are not projectiles, that an account making a child wholly a product of circumstance is the mirror error, and that mechanics is no authority on pedagogy; and the market-as-primary-driver claim on spread 11, which declines to let a monocausal story substitute for evidence. **One joint marked `leap` — the first since No. 39** (Nos. 38 and 39 carried one and two; Nos. 40 and 41 none. Marked because it is the sentence a reader most wants to be true and because the strong version is actively harmful: if gaps must vanish once barriers are removed, a persisting gap becomes proof the barriers are gone, which is deficit ideology by a longer road. It also matters that this collection defends human variation as real and not a defect, so "all difference flattens" would contradict the rest of the site. **7 documented, 3 contested, 1 leap**, verified programmatically against the file. Two prior chains had come back leap-free and their colophons flagged that as worth watching; this one fires all three tags. House zine, 12 spreads, **Star Stuff register** — and the register choice is itself the finding. **Proposed as a How We Got Here chain and rejected for it.** Every chain in that collection has a *documented handoff* between its physics and its endpoint: Bessel's apparatus reached the classroom (38), Wells's number became the guideline (39), Carnot's word arrived in Cubberley's textbook (43). **Nothing in the history of IQ or functioning labels descends from the *Theorema Egregium*** — nobody carried it there. In a chain that missing handoff would be a single `leap` joint carrying the entire argument, which the collection page's own rules forbid; in the founding register an analogy is the form rather than a defect. The zine says this on its own colophon rather than leaving it implicit. **Verified:** Gauss, *Disquisitiones generales circa superficies curvas* (1827), *Theorema Egregium* — Gaussian curvature is intrinsic, preserved under bending without stretching; a plane has K = 0 and a sphere does not, so no isometric map exists between them and no flat map preserves all distances. Cylinders and cones are developable, which is why paper takes those shapes; the folded-pizza demonstration is a standard consequence of the same theorem. Mercator (1569) is conformal — preserves angles, so a rhumb line is straight, which is what made it a navigator's tool; area distortion grows with latitude. **Tissot's indicatrix:** Nicolas Auguste Tissot introduced it in **1859**, popularised in *Mémoire sur la représentation des surfaces et les projections des cartes géographiques* (**1881**); an infinitesimal circle on the globe projects to an ellipse recording local angular and areal distortion. **Open (1):** Gauss is given from the standard mathematical statement, **not read in the original Latin**. **Open (2):** the Greenland/Africa ratio uses areas of roughly 2.2 and 30.4 million km² for a ratio near fourteen, and is **stated as approximate**. **Open (3):** the Gall–Peters controversy — Peters promoted the projection from 1973, an equivalent having been published by James Gall in 1855, and made accuracy claims cartographers rejected — is **summarised from general reference rather than the primary literature of the dispute**, and the zine states the shape of the argument rather than adjudicating it. **Deliberately not re-argued:** No. 21 *Not a Line* owns the spectrum-is-a-fingerprint case after Helen Edgar and Patrick Dwyer; this zine cites it and asks the next question — not what shape the thing is, but what a *reduction* costs. **No claim is made that the theorem proves anything about people**; spreads 7 onward are explicitly an analogy and the final spread says so ("a rhyme, not a proof"). **No chain conventions used** — no joint tags, no running spine, badge to Star Stuff — verified programmatically. **Contrast: PASS, zero failures**, screen and print, 383 elements and 65 SVG labels, with the `aria-hidden` cover numeral correct from the start this time. Markup (incl. nav placement and the badge/card rule, which caught the missing collection card on first run), sitemap and chain gates clean. |

House zine **after Helen Edgar and David Gray-Hammond** — the first piece in the series credited to two people — 9 spreads, *Star Stuff* register. **The zine's premise failed the check, and that failure became spread three.** The proposal opened on "most stars aren't solitary, they're in binary or multiple systems," which is the smoothed popular retelling and is **false**. Charles J. Lada, "Stellar Multiplicity and the Initial Mass Function: Most Stars Are Single," *ApJ* 640, L63 (2006): **two-thirds of main-sequence stellar systems in the Galactic disk are single**, because the IMF peaks among M dwarfs and multiplicity is a steep function of primary mass. Corroborated by Deepak Raghavan et al., "A Survey of Stellar Families: Multiplicity of Solar-Type Stars," *ApJS* 190, 1 (2010) — **44 ± 2%** of 454 F6–K3 primaries in multiple systems — and Gaspard Duchêne & Adam Kraus, "Stellar Multiplicity," *ARA&A* 51, 269 (2013) — **~26%** for M dwarfs, 40–50% for A–K, near-total at the top of the mass range. All three from published abstracts/reviews. The zine leads with the correction rather than burying it, and **the "Not:" set opens on refusing to promise anybody their people**. **Sibling sweep, and it found one:** [Field Guide No. 5](ways-of-being-a-star-field-guide.html) *Never Alone* carried the same error in our own work — "most stars live bound to another," closing "*Most of the sky was never single.*" Corrected the same day; see the FG 5 row and the public changelog entry. Its coords line ("~half of Sun-like stars have a partner") had been right all along, and the prose generalised from *Sun-like stars* to *stars*. **Sirius verified at the paper:** Howard E. Bond et al., "The Sirius System and Its Astrophysical Puzzles: HST and Ground-based Astrometry," *ApJ* 840, 70 (2017) — period **50.13 yr**, dynamical masses **2.063 ± 0.023** and **1.018 ± 0.011** M<sub>☉</sub>, from two decades of HST imaging plus ~2,300 historical measurements. The ~2:1 ellipse-size ratio is ours, derived from those masses, and the colophon says so. **The three-body statements are phrased to match exactly what was proved, which is narrower than the popular claim.** Bruns (1887): no first integrals beyond the classical ten that are algebraic in the coordinates and their derivatives. Poincaré (*Acta Mathematica* 13, 1890): none expressible as single-valued analytic functions — and the same work is where sensitive dependence on initial conditions was identified. Sundman (*Acta Mathematica* 36, 1912): a convergent series solution **does** exist, for non-zero angular momentum (excluding triple collision), and is useless in practice for its slow convergence. **The zine never says "the three-body problem has no solution."** The determinism/unpredictability distinction is stated explicitly and given its own refusal, because "chaos" read as "random" is the exact misuse this collection exists to answer. **David Gray-Hammond quoted verbatim, both pieces read at source, and used with his agreement (confirmed by Ryan, 2026-08-14):** the **Chaotic Self** and **neuro-anarchy** from Katie Munday & David Gray-Hammond, *Neuroqueer: Neuro-anarchy and the Chaotic Self* (NeuroHub Community, **4 April 2023**) — "our sense of Self being a fluid and moving entity, constantly changing and reshaping as we receive new information and interact with the environment" and "You can't unqueer a queer mind"; the **Autistic Rhizome** from David Gray-Hammond, *Reclaiming Neurofuturism: Rhizomatic Communities and the Chaotic Self* (NeuroHub Community, **30 April 2023**) — "communities that exist of networks with no single point of origin. They are interlinked but not dependent on one another for their existence" and "in somewhat of an unpredictable manner." **Neuro-anarchy is credited to Munday and Gray-Hammond jointly**, per this ledger's standing entry. **Helen Edgar quoted verbatim, read at source:** *My Monotropic Galaxy* (More Realms, June 2026) — **Companion Stars** is constellation **#13**, **Rhizome Array #9**, **Mycelial Stars #6**; the ordinals were checked against the full list of twenty. Her *Gravity Well* (#14) and *Dark Matter Field* (#1) are deliberately left untouched. **Territory kept separate on purpose:** No. 29 *Only in Relation* owns Barad's *intra-action* and *intradependence* — an **ontological** claim, relation before relata — so this zine confines itself to **separability**, a claim about whether a trajectory can be computed alone, and spread six says so in place rather than letting the two blur. Barad is **not cited in the argument** here. **The rhizome/three-body tension is stated as a tension, not resolved:** resilience (no node depends on another's existence) and non-separability (bound paths can't be worked out apart) are different claims, both true, and the zine says a network can be one while a cluster inside it is the other. **Open (1):** Bruns, Poincaré and Sundman are in German and French and **none has been read at the primary**; the statements are taken from standard mathematical reference works and the colophon says so. **Open (2):** ellipsoidal variability, Roche-lobe geometry, teardrop and contact configurations and mass transfer are given from **review literature, not a primary source**, and Figure 4 is labelled schematic. **Open (3):** "one orbit longer than the entire history of flowering plants" compares El-Badry, Rix & Heintz (*MNRAS* 506, 2269, 2021 — 1.3 million pairs, separations to 1 pc, periods to ~10<sup>8</sup> yr) against the **~130 Myr angiosperm fossil record**; the comparison is ours, not theirs, and is flagged in the colophon. |

Sgr A* — ring **51.8 ± 2.3 µas**, ~4 × 10⁶ M☉ at ~27,000 ly — from *ApJL* **930, L12** (2022), released 12 May 2022. That paper is **Johannsen et al.**, not Psaltis. Caught by checking the author list rather than trusting a search summary — the same failure mode as the Baldwin/King error. **Downgraded rather than shipped: the mechanism.** The usual explanation for that near-constancy — a cancellation between frame-dragging and the quadrupole structure of the Kerr metric — appeared in secondary summaries but **could not be confirmed in a primary source**, so it was **cut from the page**. The zine states the measured constancy (5 ± 0.2, ~4%) and calls it "a quirk of the equations," which is what we can stand behind. Computed here deliberately because none of the widely-circulated equivalents could be traced to a primary statement. **Given as approximate:** the ISCO periods (~30 min for Sgr A*, ~30 days for M87*), against dynamical timescales quoted in the EHT literature of ~4–56 min and ~5–61 days; the colophon gives both. **Deliberately not used, and the colophon says why:** Hawking radiation, the no-hair theorem, the information paradox and the singularity — each real, none of them the *observation* problem this piece is about. Right author, right novel, wrong typography, sourced to an aggregator and a screen adaptation. The book also supplied the beat the zine now turns on: "YES. AS PRACTICE. YOU HAVE TO START OUT LEARNING TO BELIEVE THE *LITTLE* LIES" → "YES. JUSTICE. MERCY. DUTY", which is lies-to-children arriving from the fiction side, and Death's "MY POINT EXACTLY."
