| 8 | The Ways of Migrating | 2026-08-08 | VERIFIED | Newly authored; companion catalogue to No. 30. Every headline figure traced to its primary before the entry was written, not after. **Confirmed:** Arctic tern ~71,000 km/yr, some individuals >80,000, 1.4 g geolocators on 11 birds (Egevang, Stenhouse, Phillips, Petersen & Fox, *PNAS* 107(5):2078–2081, 2010); bar-tailed godwit tag **234684**, 13,560 km, 11 days 1 hour, Alaska → Ansons Bay, Tasmania, departing 13 Oct 2022, tracked by USGS / Max Planck / USFWS — flagged in-entry as a **single-individual record, not a species norm**, and as a costly strategy (pre-migratory gut atrophy), explicitly *not* held up as superior; monarch multi-generational relay with a late-summer "super"/Methuselah generation in reproductive diapause living 8–9 months against the usual 2–6 weeks (generation counts vary by source and route — the guide says "three to five" and "several," never a fixed number); Pacific salmon geomagnetic imprinting via the 56-year Fraser River sockeye dataset (Putman, Lohmann, Putman, Quinn, Klimley & Noakes, *Current Biology* 23(4):312–316, 2013); diel vertical migration as the largest migration on Earth by biomass (~10 Gt of animals, daily), with its carbon-pump role — given as the standard characterisation rather than a single paper's claim; ballooning elicited by atmospheric-strength electric fields in still air, via mechanosensory hairs (Morley & Robert, *Current Biology* 28:2324–2330, 2018); partial migration as populations containing both migrants and residents, and described in the literature as **the commonest type, from which other types can be derived** — the load-bearing claim of the guide, and the one its neurodiversity reframe rests on; irruptive migration as food-driven, non-annual and directionally variable (cone-crop failure, lemming cycles); European eel Sargasso migration, 5,000–10,000 km at 3–12 km/day, **first direct tracking evidence 2022** (Wright, Piper, Aarestrup et al., *Scientific Reports* 12:15362); postglacial tree range shift ~60–550 m/yr and Reid's paradox (Reid, 1899), with rare long-distance dispersal as the leading explanation; Saharan dust ~182 Mt/yr leaving west Africa, ~27.7 Mt/yr reaching the Amazon basin carrying ~22,000 t of phosphorus, CALIPSO 2007–2013 (Yu et al., *Geophysical Research Letters*, 2015) — flagged in-entry as estimates with large interannual variability, not a ledger; radial migration / churning in the galactic disc via transient spiral corotation resonances (Sellwood & Binney, *MNRAS*, 2002) and the now-standard consequence that most solar-neighbourhood stars, likely including the Sun, formed at other radii — the guide says "probably" and "in all likelihood," and never asserts a birth radius for the Sun. **The reframe is ours:** that "migration" was handed to us as a single shape, and that a population holding both migrants and residents is the norm rather than the anomaly. **Held with care, stated twice on the page (intro band and coda):** this guide reads migration as a way of being and is *not* a frame for human displacement, which is coerced and political; the sole human entry is our own community's (late identification, unmasking) and describes a crossing nobody is driven into. |
| 9 | A Field Guide to Being a Shark | 2026-08-10 | VERIFIED | First check, done during the build. Companion catalogue to Nos. 32–34, written for the same young person. Twelve entries, each with its load-bearing figure traced to a primary or authoritative source before it was written. **Verified:** whale shark max ~18.8 m; dwarf lanternshark ~20 cm, with a 28.9 cm individual reported from a 2021 survey (the guide gives ~20 cm and notes the record). Greenland shark ages and swim speed from Nielsen et al. (2016) and Watanabe et al. (2012) — already checked for No. 34. Goblin shark: Nakaya et al. (2016), *Scientific Reports* 6:27786 — jaws projected at up to **3.1 m/s** and to **8.6–9.4% of total length**, the fastest and greatest protrusion among sharks, via a double set of ligaments at the mandibular joints. Thresher: Oliver, Turner, Gann, Silvosa & D'Urban Jackson (2013), *PLoS ONE* 8(7):e67380 — **25 hunting events** analysed from 61 observations off the Philippines, Jun–Oct 2010; overhead tail-slaps stunning multiple sardines, forceful enough to cavitate. Epaulette shark: walks on paired fins in and out of water; cerebral blood flow unaffected by **2 hours** of severe hypoxia; survives near-anoxia via metabolic suppression. Cookiecutter: ventral photophores for counterillumination and an unlit dark collar, following **Widder (1998)**, *Environmental Biology of Fishes*. Bonnethead: Leigh, Papastamatiou & German (2018), *Proc. R. Soc. B* 285(1886):20181583 — seagrass **up to 62.1% of gut content mass**, digested at **~50% efficiency**, fibre and soluble carbohydrate both; first shark shown to be an omnivore. Zebra shark: Dudgeon et al. (2017), *Scientific Reports* 7:40537 — Leonie at Reef HQ, Queensland, switching from sexual reproduction to parthenogenesis after three years without a male. **The entry that required reading the primary rather than the popular account:** the great hammerhead. The widespread story is that the cephalofoil supercharges electroreception. **Kajiura & Holland tested it and found no increase in electrosensory sensitivity to prey-like fields** — what the head confers is a wider lateral search area and better manoeuvrability, with **McComb et al. (2009)**, *J. Exp. Biol.* 212, 4010, showing enhanced binocular overlap and a much larger visual field. The guide says *wider, not sharper*, and names the flattering misreading as the same reflex that turns unusual into deficit, pointed the other way. **Held honestly** (stated in the footer): maximum lengths are records, not typical animals; the cookiecutter's dark collar as a lure is the leading explanation rather than a settled one; and the cephalofoil is agreed to serve several overlapping functions, so "wider, not sharper" is one well-tested finding and not the whole account. The wobbegong entry deliberately carries **no strike-duration figure** — a widely repeated ~100 ms number could only be traced to secondary sources, so the prose says "a sudden snap" instead. **The frilled shark entry is about a phrase, not an animal.** "Living fossil" is not a fact about *Chlamydoselachus*: no lineage stops, and the accurate statement is that this one has stayed well matched to a deep, stable niche. It is cross-linked to No. 32's demolition of *primitive*, and named there as the same move in a friendlier coat. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 12 entries render; **0 SVG `<text>` nodes below 4.5:1**, confirmed with the shape-aware audit built for the site-wide sweep. Diagrams checked by eye at render size, which caught three things no automated check would: the shark silhouette helper first drew a thin lens with a detached floating triangle and read as a paper dart, not an animal (redrawn with a fuller fusiform body, a dorsal based on the back, a pectoral and a notched tail); the whale shark faced **away** from the plankton streaming past its tail (turned to face its food); and the thresher's strike arc left the **middle of the body** rather than the tail, which is the entire point of the entry (re-anchored to the tail). |
| 10 | A Field Guide to Being a Turtle | 2026-08-11 | VERIFIED | First check, done during the build. Companion catalogue to **No. 35**, built from research gathered for that zine and logged as a planned piece in Open items. **12 animal entries + 5 "turtles people made."** **Read in the primary / verified:** leatherback — dives recorded to ~**1,280 m**; carapace is hundreds of interlocking ossicles in oil-saturated skin rather than bony plates with scutes; body temperature held up to ~**18 °C** above ambient (≈25 °C internal in ≈7 °C water) by **gigantothermy** — large body, peripheral insulation, circulatory control — following Paladino, O'Connor & Spotila (*Nature* 344, 1990) and the leatherback thermal-strategy literature; largest range of any reptile. *Carettochelys insculpta* — only freshwater turtle with true flippers; sole living member of **Carettochelyidae**; northern Australia and southern New Guinea; shell over 70 cm. *Chelus fimbriatus* — Lemell et al., "Feeding patterns of *Chelus fimbriatus*" (*J. Exp. Biol.* 205, 2002) and Lemell et al. (*Amphibia-Reptilia* 31, 2010): hyoid apparatus about two-thirds the skull's length, **jaw adductors poorly developed relative to other turtles**, tongue **nearly reduced**, distensible oesophagus, prey inhaled rather than seized. *Macrochelys temminckii* — vermiform lingual lure; four phases of predation (waiting, luring, attack, prey-handling) from the neonate luring study; the **only living reptile known to carry a predatory lure inside the mouth**. *Rheodytes leukops* — Priest (1997) and Priest & Franklin (2002): up to ~**70%** of total oxygen uptake via two enlarged cloacal bursae, papillae increasing surface area ~**16-fold**. *Pelodiscus sinensis* — Ip, Loong, Lee, Ong, Wong & Chew, "The Chinese soft-shelled turtle, *Pelodiscus sinensis*, excretes urea mainly through the mouth instead of the kidney" (*J. Exp. Biol.* 215, 3723–3733, 2012); ~50× more urea leaving by that route than via the cloaca. *Podocnemis expansa* — **Ferrara, Vogt & Sousa-Lima**, "Turtle vocalizations as the first evidence of posthatching parental care in chelonians" (*J. Comp. Psychol.*, 2013) and "Sound communication and social behavior in an Amazonian river turtle" (*Herpetologica*, 2014): **2,122 sounds in 11 types**, from hatchlings in eggs, in open nests and in the river, and from adult females basking and nesting, with females approaching and responding to hatchling sounds in the river. *Chrysemys picta* hatchlings — Costanzo, Lee and colleagues: overwinter in shallow natal nests where temperatures fall below **−10 °C**; freeze tolerance limited to about **−4 °C**; survival therefore rests on **supercooling**, which soil ice nuclei active between about −3.5 and −5 °C defeat on contact. *Gopherus agassizii* — up to **95%** of life underground, hibernating up to nine months; **keystone species**, burrows used by other wildlife (USFWS; Desert Tortoise Council). *Chelodina steindachneri* — Pilbara/Gascoyne, aestivates buried in riverbed or bank through dry periods of a year or more (Legler & Georges, *Fauna of Australia* Vol. 2A). *Chelonia mydas* — natal homing per Brothers & Lohmann (*Current Biology* 25, 2015); largely herbivorous as adults. *Rafetus swinhoei* — last known female died at **Suzhou, April 2019**, in recovery from anaesthesia after artificial insemination; a second female confirmed genetically at **Đồng Mô, Vietnam (2020)** was found dead **April 2023**. **Held honestly:** maxima and depth records are records, not typical animals. **The *Rafetus* count is deliberately given as a range ("two or three")** because published counts disagree and keep moving — flagged on the card and in the footer as a figure to re-check rather than quote, exactly the failure mode CLAUDE.md warns about for numbers that go stale in a reference page. Cloacal respiration is **bimodal**, not a lung replacement; the ~70% figure is aquatic uptake under study conditions, and the widely circulated "100%" and "submerged for weeks" claims are **not** supported by those papers and are not used. Painted turtle hatchlings *are* moderately freeze tolerant — supercooling is the primary strategy, not the only one. The Arrau result establishes that these turtles vocalise and that females respond to hatchlings; **it does not establish what any call means**, and the entry says so and declines to translate. The reframe — that an order this varied has no standard member, and that an assumed silence indexes the listener at least as often as the speaker — is **ours**, and is marked as a rhyme rather than a proof. **Structural decision, and the reason for it:** the five "turtles people made" (Turtle Island; *ao* in the *Huainanzi*; Kurma; Great A'Tuin; the Larrakia Star Dreaming) are in a **separate section with a different card type** — dashed border, no "sounds like / actually" table, a whose-it-is / what-it-does / what-gets-got-wrong list instead. Running a deficit-reframe device over somebody's cosmology would be obscene, and a field guide's native move — compressing things into comparable units — is precisely what flattens a knowledge system into a motif. The format difference is stated in the intro band as the argument. **The fifth card declines to be a card:** the Star Dreaming is Oldman Juma Fejo's, of the Larrakia People, shared in Yunkaporta's *Sand Talk* (2019); the card gives provenance, notes that the invitation to share is real and published, states that an invitation to share is not an invitation to *paraphrase*, points at the quoted passages in No. 35 and at the book, and gives no summary. **Kurma is flagged hardest** because we nearly made the world-bearer error ourselves from secondary summaries while building No. 35. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 629 text elements composited against real ancestor backgrounds, **min 4.63:1, zero AA failures**; under emulated print **min 12.22:1, zero failures**. Mobile verified at 390px, no horizontal overflow. **All 17 diagrams inspected at render size** via headless-Chrome captures of the live page, which caught three faults no automated check sees: the mata mata's suction funnel was drawn **apex-first toward the prey**, i.e. saying the fish is expelled from a point rather than drawn into a gape — direction is the entire content of that figure; the alligator snapper was a featureless mound with a squiggle on it, with **no jaws, so nothing read as a mouth or a turtle**, now drawn as an open gape hinged at the left with the lure inside and a fish approaching; and two labels on the painted-turtle card overlapped at 8px spacing. A fourth fault was caught by measurement rather than eye: the house ember `#c1653a`, used as the *Rafetus* entry colour, drives `.ls-coords-value` on the tinted panel at **4.33:1** — under the small-text floor — and was lifted to `#d97848` (5.61:1) in the same hue family. Each fix carries a comment in the source saying what went wrong. |
| 11 | A Field Guide to Being a Tortoise | 2026-08-11 | VERIFIED | First check, done during the build. Sibling to **No. 10**; scope is **Testudinidae**, the land tortoises. **Scope decision:** tortoises *are* turtles taxonomically, so the guide states the usage problem in its intro band rather than pretending otherwise — American English treats *turtle* as the whole order, British English splits *tortoise* / *turtle* / *terrapin* — and says plainly that neither usage is the taxonomy. **The Mojave desert tortoise was deliberately not reused** from No. 10; the gopher tortoise carries the burrow/keystone entry here instead, and the two are cross-linked. **Read in the primary / verified:** **Zond 5** — launched 15 September 1968 from Baykonur with a biological payload including two steppe tortoises (*Testudo horsfieldii*), passed ~**1,950 km** above the lunar far side on 18 September, splashed down in the Indian Ocean 21 September after a guidance error; both tortoises survived, lost roughly **10%** of body weight, and were reported in good health after examination in Moscow (NASA NSSDCA record 1968-076A and the Soviet mission reporting). Apollo 8 carried the first humans around the Moon in December 1968, so the priority claim — **first animals to circle the Moon and return** — holds. **Giant tortoise predation** — **Gerlach, J. & Zora, A.**, "Giant tortoises hunt and consume birds," *Current Biology* 31(17), published 23 August 2021: an adult female *Aldabrachelys gigantea* on **Frégate Island**, Seychelles, filmed on **30 July 2020** pursuing a tern chick along a log and swallowing it whole over about **seven minutes**; described as the first documented deliberate hunting by a wild tortoise. Filmed by Zora; Gerlach is at Peterhouse, Cambridge. **Gopher tortoise** — burrows over **14 m** long and **3 m** deep; commonly cited as used by **more than 350 species**; Florida FWC and the gopher-tortoise literature. **Pancake tortoise** — flattened, flexible, **fenestrated** carapace bone; wedges into rock crevices using the forelimbs; reported as the fastest tortoise and the best climber. **Speckled padloper** (*Chersobius signatus*) — males 6–8 cm, females to nearly 10 cm straight carapace length, 95–165 g, Little Namaqualand, South Africa; the world's smallest tortoise. **Galápagos** — saddleback carapaces on drier, lower islands where browse is held above ground (*Opuntia*), domed carapaces in wetter highlands with abundant ground vegetation; twelve species considered extant after Lonesome George's death. **Jonathan** — Seychelles giant tortoise on St Helena; arrived 1882 as a gift to the governor, estimated at ~50 years old then, so hatching is put at circa **1832**; Guinness World Records lists him as the oldest living land animal; reports of his death in early 2026 were false. **Indian star tortoise** — more than **34,000** live animals reported confiscated 2000–2015, over **21,300** within India; **2,098** seized from one dinghy by the Sri Lankan Navy, June 2017; roughly **11%** of global chelonian seizures. **Ploughshare tortoise** (*Astrochelys yniphora*, angonoka) — Baly Bay, Madagascar; shells engraved by conservation staff to reduce their value to collectors, a measure conservationists report has **not** clearly deterred poaching. **Lonesome George** died **24 June 2012**. **Fernanda** — found on Fernandina 2019; genome sequencing published in *Communications Biology* (2022) by Princeton, Yale and Ecuadorian researchers confirmed her as *Chelonoidis phantasticus*, genetically and morphologically distinct from all other Galápagos species and closely related to the male specimen collected in **1906**, ruling out hybrid or stray origin. **Île aux Aigrettes** — **26** Aldabra giant tortoises introduced 2000–2011 as ecological replacements for the extinct Mauritian *Cylindraspis inepta*; minimum **529 hatchlings** in 11 years, low mortality, enhanced germination and growth of large-seeded natives, and grazed clearings colonised by a native tussock grass the tortoises don't eat. **Three claims deliberately corrected, hedged, or excluded — each named on the page:** *(1)* **The pancake tortoise does not inflate itself.** The claim that it wedges into crevices by inflating "chuckwalla style" is repeated in decades of popular accounts and is now believed to be untrue; the wedging is done with the limbs. The entry states the correction rather than dropping it quietly, and pairs it with the Aldabra hunting result as two cases of a confident statement about an animal turning out to be a statement about us. *(2)* **The star tortoise's pattern as disruptive camouflage is the standard account, not a finding we can cite a test for**, and is worded that way on the card and in the footer. *(3)* **Adwaita's frequently quoted ~255 years is not established and is not used**, and the footer says so alongside the note that Jonathan's 1832 is a back-calculation and not a record. Also flagged: gopher-tortoise commensal counts vary between studies and are given as "more than 350" rather than an exact census; ecological replacement is **contested conservation practice, not settled policy**, and the Mauritius programme is presented as one well-documented case rather than a general licence. **Held honestly:** the Lonesome George entry explicitly rejects the "loneliest animal in the world" framing as a description of how *we* felt rather than of the animal, while stating that the loss is not thereby smaller. The Zond 5 entry notes that nobody asked the tortoises. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 527 text elements composited against real ancestor backgrounds, **min 5.57:1, zero AA failures**; under emulated print **min 12.30:1, zero failures**. Mobile verified at 390px, no horizontal overflow. All 12 diagrams inspected at render size and checked programmatically for out-of-bounds and overlapping labels, which caught **seven** label faults across three cards — the Galápagos captions ran past the viewBox edge and into each other, and the Île aux Aigrettes captions overflowed the left edge and collided. The **tortoise glyph is drawn deliberately unlike No. 10's turtle glyph** — high domed shell rather than flat oval, columnar legs going straight down rather than limbs splayed to the sides — so the two guides are distinguishable at ~90px; a note in the source says so. |

Spread-3's note **declines to claim Helen meant a stellar stream** and says so in the text. **The index map is the interesting failure and it was caught by eye, not by a gate.** Drawn first in tidy numeric rows, it put the 4→20 line across half the figure and ran the dashed L★S group line **straight through points 11 and 12**, asserting a membership Helen never states. Redrawn by relationship so every connected pair is adjacent and no line passes through a point it does not join; a comment at the coordinates records why. **This is the third time a diagram fault has passed every automated check on this site** (see No. 20's broadside and No. 46's Figure 4) — `check-contrast.mjs` measures whether a label is legible, never whether the drawing is true. **Contrast: PASS, zero failures**, screen and print, 417 screen and 386 print elements with 86 SVG labels — eight glyph numerals initially failed at 4.36–4.43:1 from an `opacity="0.75"`, which is exactly the fault the 2026-08-13 sweep cleared site-wide; opacity removed rather than the colour lightened. Markup, sitemap and chain gates clean. **Permissions — raised as a gating item and cleared before shipping.** The source blog post is an edited and expanded version of Helen's forthcoming chapter in *UNIQUE*, so verbatim republication of all twenty entries needed her explicit yes rather than an assumption of goodwill. The build was **held unpushed** until **Helen confirmed, relayed by Ryan on 2026-08-14**; only then does the footer's "Reproduced with her permission" become a true statement, which is why it was written before the confirmation but not published before it. **Open (1):** the astronomical notes behind the folds are given from standard references rather than primary sources read for this page, and the footer says so. **Open (2):** the *UNIQUE* call for contributions is described as open **at the time of Helen's essay** (June 2026) rather than as open now — the page words it that way deliberately, since we have no way to check the book's current status and a stale open call is a small cruelty to send someone toward. |
| 13 | A Field Guide to Watching Animals | 2026-08-14 | VERIFIED (four open items) | First check, done during the build. Sixteen entries, filed in *Easter Eggs* as the companion catalogue to No. 47 rather than in Field Guides, because it embeds comedy videos and the tonal call in No. 47 applies. **Video identifiers were extracted from Ze Frank's own playlist in a browser and then diffed against the file programmatically, not written from memory** — all 16 ids are 11 characters and every id/title pair matches the extraction exactly. This is the single largest factual risk on a page like this: a wrong id is a dead embed that still looks plausible, and a *plausible* wrong link is worse than a broken one. **Twelve entries restate claims already checked for the piece they pair with**, and the citation lives in that piece's row here rather than being duplicated: mantis shrimp wavelength discrimination (Thoen et al., *Science*, 2014) at No. 15; the octopus, jellyfish, slime mould, corvid (Olkowicz et al., 2016), elephant and jumping-spider entries at Field Guide No. 7; carcinisation/decarcinisation counts (Wolfe, Luque & Bracken-Grissom, *BioEssays*, 2021) at No. 31; tesserae (Dean & Summers, *Zoology*, 2006) and *Minjinia turgenensis* (Brazeau et al., *Nature Ecology & Evolution*, 2020) at No. 32; the tardigrade tun at Field Guide No. 3; acacia ants at Field Guide No. 6; niche construction at No. 16. **Four are new to this site:** aye-aye percussive foraging from C. J. Erickson (1991; 1994), including the result that cavities backfilled with gelatin or acoustic foam were still located and excavated, so reverberation in an air-filled cavity is not necessary; Cuvierian tubule expulsion and regeneration from VandenSpiegel & Jangoux on *Holothuria forskali* (*Biological Bulletin*, 2000) — waves of 10–30 tubules at ~10-day intervals, ~5 weeks per tubule; nudibranch kleptocnidae sequestered in cnidosacs within cnidophages; and hedgehog self-anointing. **The reframe table was pointed deliberately.** It corrects the popular verdict on the *animal* and never anything in the videos — because No. 47 argues the series does not issue verdicts, so a table aimed at the videos would contradict the zine it is a companion to. The page states that on its face. **One entry has no row aimed at a belief:** the aye-aye is killed in parts of Madagascar for what it is held to mean, and running "sounds like / actually" over somebody's cosmology is the error Field Guide No. 10 refused with its five turtles people made — so the table there answers claims about the animal's body, and the killings are stated plainly outside it. **Open:** (1) **hedgehog self-anointing is the thinnest entry and says so** — the behaviour is well documented, the function is unresolved, the sources we could reach on specifics were popular rather than primary, so the entry lists the competing hypotheses, picks none, and deliberately cites no study; (2) nudibranch kleptocnidae follows review literature rather than a single primary paper read end to end, and is phrased no more strongly; (3) Erickson's papers are cited from their publication records rather than read in full; (4) links depend on third-party hosting and will break honestly rather than silently point elsewhere if a video is removed or retitled. Screen and print contrast measured (614 elements) after clearing a **+bg warning of 16 elements** caused by a literal `rgba(15,15,42,0.5)` on the footer panel, replaced with the `--deep` token so it inverts on paper. Markup, sitemap, sheet and search-index gates clean; 16 records at 100% coverage. **Ze Frank has no involvement and was not asked.** |
| 14 | A Field Guide to the Ways of Being Alone | 2026-08-16 | CORRECTED → VERIFIED (five open items) | **2026-08-16, hours after shipping — the guide credited an organisation where it owed a person.** As published it said it grew from "the Stimpunks page" *Lone Wolfing: The Joys of Autistic Solitude*, in five places. The essay is by **Betsy Selvam**, Stimpunks board member (stimpunks.org, 18 June 2023, CC BY-SA 4.0), and the byline was on the page throughout — the WebFetch during research returned the article's argument and citations and was never asked who wrote it. Caught by **Ryan Boren**. Corrected in the guide (intro band, disperser entry, footer, meta description and JSON-LD), the index card, the collection page's drift paragraph, `changelog.html` and this row. **Why it happened, since it is a house-specific trap:** every other source on the guide is a named human — Rubin, Crane, Fisher, Temeles, Kummer, Aureli, Kocher, Elbroch, Danforth — and the one that got flattened into an institution is the one that came from inside. *A collectively-run site is exactly where an author disappears into the masthead, and "ours" is not a citation.* **Standing rule taken from it:** when a piece grows from a stimpunks.org or morerealms.com page, fetch the byline as a separate question before writing the credit line — the same discipline already applied to outside sources. **A second slip inside the same correction, also caught by Ryan Boren:** the first pass stamped the essay **CC BY-NC 4.0**. That licence belongs to Betsy Selvam's *header image*; her **writing carries the house licence, CC BY-SA 4.0**. The research fetch had reported the NC licence accurately — as an image credit — and it was widened to the whole post on the way into the footer. *A licence printed beside artwork covers the artwork.* Corrected in the guide's footer, the changelog entry and this row. Two attribution errors on one source in one day is the argument for treating credit lines as claims that need checking rather than as courtesy text. First check, done during the build. Twelve entries plus a thirteenth card, grown from **Betsy Selvam**'s *Lone Wolfing: The Joys of Autistic Solitude*. **The organising rubric is sourced, not invented, and the guide says so.** It came out of Selvam's essay rather than from independent reading: The four conditions are **Kenneth Rubin's**, quoted in **Brent Crane, "The Virtues of Isolation," *The Atlantic*, 30 March 2017** — solitude is productive only if voluntary, if emotion can be regulated within it, if one can rejoin when desired, and if relationships outside it are maintained. Crane's "rejuvenation / suffering … and the ability to come back" gloss is quoted in Selvam's essay, which is where this guide found it. **Rubin's conditions are a claim about human solitude; using them to sort animals is this guide's framing device and is marked on the page as a rhyme rather than as evidence.** Claims verified: **wolf dispersal** at 9–36 months with secondary dispersal between packs (Mech & Boitani, *Wolves*, Chicago 2003; Morales-González et al., *Biological Reviews* 97, 2022); **social bees under 10% of all bee species** — this is the authors' own framing in Princeton University Press's description of **Danforth, Minckley & Neff, *The Solitary Bees* (2019)**, and is stated as "fewer than one in ten" rather than as a derived "90%" count; **halictid social polymorphism** with common-garden retention of each population's behaviour (Kocher et al., *Nature Communications* 9:4338, 2018; genome in *Genome Biology* 14:R142, 2013); **panda scent-marking** — over 90% longest-surviving AGS marks, over 80% oriented toward animal trails, maintaining a network among animals with little direct contact; **dear enemy** (Fisher 1954) **with the nasty-neighbour inverse** (Temeles, *Animal Behaviour* 47, 1994) kept because both directions are real; **cheetah** solitary females, lifelong male coalitions, ~30% of Serengeti coalition members unrelated, coalition territories under 10% of a female's home range; **fission-fusion** named by Kummer (1971) and formalised by Aureli et al., *Current Anthropology* 49(4), 2008; **Magicicada** 13/17-year cycles, five nymphal instars, predator satiation; **brown bear** neighbourhood fidelity at salmon streams and Brooks Falls tolerance under abundance; **puma reciprocity** — 1,000+ carcasses, tolerance not predicted by kinship, 7.7× more likely to share with a past sharer (Elbroch, Levy, Lubell, Quigley & Caragiulo, *Science Advances* 3(10):e1701218, 2017); **orangutan** party size ~1.6–1.9 in West Sumatra against ~1.05–1.3 in East Sumatra and Borneo, tracking fruit availability rather than species and revising the older Sumatran/Bornean contrast; **wandering albatross** ~280-day chick-rearing, biennial breeding with a sabbatical year, ~0.3% divorce, bonds taking 3.2 (M) and 2.3 (F) years to form; **Octopolis/Octlantis** dimensions, den counts and animal counts (Scheel, Godfrey-Smith et al., *Marine and Freshwater Behaviour and Physiology*). **One finding was deliberately refused rather than used.** The Kocher paper identifies a variant in the intron of ***syntaxin 1a*, an autism-associated gene**, as associated with the solitary/social polymorphism. It is the single most quotable thing in the source material and it is named in the page's refusals as a trap rather than carried into any argument — a bee's nesting strategy is not our social life, and running that inference is the naturalistic fallacy with a citation attached, which is precisely what *How We Got Here* exists to argue against. **The thirteenth card carries no reframe table, and that is a third distinct reason for withholding it**, added to the two the collection already had: not a cosmology (Field Guide No. 10) and not an absence of verdict (No. 12), but a verdict that is *not a misreading of the animal* — "octopuses are secretly social" is a wish about us laid over a shortage of hard substrate, and a sounds-like/actually row has nothing to say to a reader who got the animal right. **Open:** (1) the **15–20% lone-wolf share** is a widely reported approximation that varies by population and by how floaters are counted, and is worded as approximate; (2) the **~20,000 described bee species** total is given as roughly, and the under-10% figure is cited to the book's own description rather than to a page read in the book itself; (3) **Swaisgood's panda work** is cited as a body of research plus Zhou et al. (*Journal of Zoology*, 2019) rather than one paper read end to end; (4) **Caro's Serengeti cheetah fieldwork** is cited as the foundational source from its publication record, not read in full; (5) the reading of the octopus sites as **housing shortage rather than society is an interpretation**, stated as such in the footer — the defensible claim is only that dense aggregation on scarce hard substrate does not by itself demonstrate sociality. Screen and print contrast measured (1,164 elements, 56 SVG labels), 0 failures in both media after clearing a **+bg warning of 8 elements** caused by a literal `rgba(15,15,42,0.6)` on the four condition chips, fixed with a print override rather than by changing the screen design. Overlap gate clean at 1,346 text boxes. Markup, sitemap and chain gates clean; 13 entries render, 12 tables, deep links open. **Helen Edgar's "Reclaiming Rest: Autistic Burnout, Monotropism, and Resistance" is cited for solitude-as-recovery; this is a house guide, not a guest one, and she has not reviewed it.** |
| The Constellation Sheet | 2026-08-14 | VERIFIED (no factual claims) | **A blank sheet, and the ledger row exists to record that it makes no claims rather than to check any.** The companion surface to Field Guide No. 12, which ends by asking readers to draw their own constellations. Its only assertions are about itself — 80 stars, 0 lines, ten index rows — and each was verified by counting the rendered elements rather than the source. **It fails the Print collection's first rule on purpose:** a broadside compresses an argument made at length elsewhere and inherits that piece's fact-checking; this compresses nothing, so `collection-print.html` now describes the rack as eight broadsides plus **two** pieces that are not broadsides, joining `shorthand-evolution.html` under the same exemption. **Two editorial decisions that are the substance of the piece.** (1) The title carries a blank rule where Helen's reads *Monotropic* — her word for her own experience, and a sheet handed to a stranger must not assume it of them. (2) Side B has **ten** rows rather than twenty, because twenty is her number, Field Guide No. 12 says so on its face, and a printed twenty would convert a personal map into a quota. **The engineering decision is the load-bearing one and it was verified by looking, not by inference.** The site's starfield is a CSS radial-gradient on `body::before`; browsers omit background graphics by default, which is why 44 pages here once printed blank. On this sheet that failure mode would produce an empty white box, so every star is an SVG `<circle>` — content, not decoration. **Rendered to PDF at both papers with `printBackground: false`, the reader's default, and the pages were read back and inspected**: all 80 stars, the violet and cyan registration bars, and both rule sets print. Spot colours reuse the two already darkened for paper on the-same-water broadside — violet `#5b21b6` (9.0:1 on white) and cyan `#164e63` (9.1:1) — declared literally rather than aliased, because `starstuff.css` flattens `--sp-*` to `#111111` in print by design. **The scatter is generated once from a fixed seed and pasted in literally**, never at page load: a print artifact must be the same object in every copy, or two people cannot discuss the same star. **`check-sheets.mjs` caught two faults no other gate could see**, both of which clip silently rather than paginating — side B overran its box by 104px at twelve rows, and side A by 7px after the field was enlarged; the field's viewBox was then trimmed to 534 units, with the lowest star edge measured at 526.9 to confirm nothing was cut. Final: **2 printed sides on Letter and 2 on A4**, 0 overflow. Contrast, markup, sitemap and chain gates clean. **No sources to check** — the only external reference is Helen Edgar's essay and the *UNIQUE* call, both already verified in the Field Guide No. 12 row above, and the *UNIQUE* call is described as open *at the time of her essay* rather than as open now. |
| 15 | A Field Guide to Nests | 2026-08-16 | VERIFIED (six open items) | First check, done during the build. House guide, 13 entries plus a fourteenth card with no reframe table. **Built as the deliberate companion to No. 55, and designed around not repeating it.** No. 55 asks *how does a nest get built*; this asks *what counts as one*, and the finding is the guide's own: **most nests are not built** — they are borrowed, inherited, induced in another organism, secreted from the body, or are the body. **The sort into five kinds of labour is ours, and arrived after the entries rather than before them** — we began sorting by material, and the material sort failed on the hermit crab and the gall wasp, both of which supply none. That derivation is stated on the page rather than presented as a scheme. **Verified at primary or at the journal:** Kawase, H., Okata, Y. & Ito, K., "Role of huge geometric circular structures in the reproduction of a marine pufferfish," *Scientific Reports* **3**, 2106 (2013) — male *Torquigener albomaculosus* ≈10 cm, structure ≈2 m, and the radial geometry gathers fine sand into the centre, which bears on female choice; the species was named in 2014, after the paper. Ocko, S. A., King, H., Andreen, D., Bardunias, P., Turner, J. S., Soar, R. & Mahadevan, L., "Solar-powered ventilation of African termite mounds," *J. Exp. Biol.* **220**(18), 3260–3269 (2017) — **the word *uninhabited* is the paper's own**, describing mounds enclosing broad tunnels that protrude above the termites' *subterranean* nests, with diurnal solar heating driving convection; measured in *Macrotermes michaelseni*, compared to *Odontotermes obesus*. Chase, I. D., Weissburg, M. & DeWitt, T. H., "The vacancy chain process," *Animal Behaviour* (1988), with Lewis, S. M. & Rotjan, R. D., *Ethology* **115** (2009) confirming that benefits are **distributed across many chain participants**; synchronous chains have crabs queuing beside a vacant shell in *descending size order*. Sorenson, M. D. & Payne, R. B. (2002) — obligate interspecific brood parasitism evolved independently **at least seven times**: three in cuckoos, one in honeyguides, one in waterfowl (*Heteronetta atricapilla*), two in songbirds (Viduidae, *Molothrus*); ~99% of birds provide parental care. Mason, L. D., Wardell-Johnson, G. & Main, B. Y., *Pacific Conservation Biology* (2018) — female *Gaius villosus* "Number 16," North Bungulla Reserve near Tammin, Western Australia, ~43 years, the longest-lived spider recorded, killed by a parasitic wasp rather than by age. Endler, J. A., Endler, L. C. & Doerr, N. R., "Great bowerbirds create theaters with forced perspective when seen by their audience," *Current Biology* **20**, 1679–1684 (2010). Largest bird nest: bald eagles near St Petersburg, Florida, 2.9 m × 6 m, examined 1963, >2 tonnes (Guinness World Records). Gall induction: inducers deliver effectors including phytohormones that redirect host development, and **different insect species induce distinct gall morphologies on the same host**, which is the evidence that the design is the insect's — the literature's own term is *extended phenotype*, argued most explicitly in Hearn et al., "Genomic dissection of an extended phenotype: oak galling by a cynipid gall wasp," *PLOS Genetics* (2019). **The Number 16 entry deliberately relocates its own headline.** The circulating fact is the spider's age; the finding is that **Barbara York Main** began following that population in **1974** and kept returning for over forty years, which is the only reason a whole spider life fits inside a study. Recorded because "longest-lived spider" is exactly the shape of a fact that travels while the work behind it disappears. **The fourteenth card carries no reframe table, and this is the fourth distinct reason on the site for withholding the apparatus**, after FG 10 (a cosmology cannot take it), FG 12 (no verdict to correct) and FG 14 (a verdict that is a wish rather than a misreading). Here: the subject is the reader's own room, and running *sounds like / actually* over it would make us the observer in the doorway that No. 55 spends ten spreads indicting — plus No. 55's own refusal that *not every mess is a nest* means a table would have to sort a nest from its burnout ruins on sight, which nobody can do. **Deliberately out of scope:** the **beaver**, already the niche-construction case in *Minor Worlds* and *Watching Animals*, so re-carding it would be a third telling; and the weaver, present in one hand-off card rather than a full entry, per Ryan's call when the guide was proposed. **Open:** (1) caddisfly case-building, (2) tailorbird stitching and paper-wasp fibre, (3) woodpecker cavity supply and the secondary-cavity-nester guild, (4) emperor penguin brooding and huddle rotation, and (5) the swiftlet's saliva nest and its commercial harvest are all given as **well-established natural history rather than from papers read for this guide**, and no precise figures are printed for any of them — the footer says so; (6) the *Science* paper behind the weaver hand-off card was read at abstract level only, as No. 55's colophon also states. Gall-inducer richness is printed as a range (21,000–211,000 estimated, ~13,000 described) rather than a number. **The ninth guide to arrive independently at *there is no standard nest*** — counted by grep across the guides, not from memory. Six gates clean, after two real faults the gates caught: **two nested `<a>` inside card descriptions** on `index.html` and `collection-field-guides.html` (the fault `check-markup.mjs` exists for, and a repeat of a known trap — the card wrapper is already an anchor), and **two SVG labels clipped outside their viewBox** in the vacancy-chain and rooms diagrams, both moved rather than shrunk. |
| FG 16 | A Field Guide to Eggs | 2026-08-17 | VERIFIED (six open items) | First check, done during the build. House guide, 15 entries plus a sixteenth card with no reframe table, filed in *Field Guides*. **The deliberate companion to No. 57, built to not repeat it:** that zine asks *what does a shell do* and answers that an eggshell hands most of its calcium to the skeleton of the animal it contains; this asks the prior question, *what counts as an egg*, and finds the answer far wider than one hen could carry. **The sort into five distances from the body is ours and arrived after the entries, not before them** — two earlier sorts were tried and abandoned, and the page says so on its own face: a *material* sort collapsed because a hen's egg and a common murre's are the same object by every material measure (and the interesting thing about the murre is a wrong explanation of its shape, not its chemistry), and a *who-pays-for-the-embryo* sort put several animals in two gates at once. **Verified at primary, read at source, open access:** Halgrain, M., Georgeault, S., Bernardet, N., Hincke, M. T. & Réhault-Godbert, S., *Frontiers in Physiology* **13**, 838013 (2022) — thickness EID0→EID15 not significantly different (*p* = 0.110), breaking strength EID11→EID15 significantly reduced (*p* = 0.007), shell supplying >80% of embryonic calcium, skeleton holding ~75% at hatch; the same figures No. 57 carries, and the hen card hands its argument to that zine rather than re-running it. Robison, B. H., Seibel, B. & Drazen, J., "Deep-sea octopus (*Graneledone boreopacifica*) conducts the longest-known egg-brooding period of any animal," *PLOS ONE* **9**(7): e103437 (2014) — an individually recognisable female at ~1,400 m in Monterey Canyon, first seen May 2007, revisited **18 times**, brooding **53 months**, never observed feeding. **Verified at journal or abstract level, and marked as such on the page:** squamate viviparity has evolved **at least 115 times**, against roughly 140 origins across all vertebrates, in nearly 20% of species — Pyron, R. A. & Burbrink, F. T., *Ecology Letters* **17**, 13–21 (2014); **the reversibility of the transition is contested and is left open on the card**, with Blackburn, D. G., "Evolution of viviparity in squamate reptiles: reversibility reconsidered," *J. Exp. Zool. B* (2015) cited against it — we print the count, which is not the disputed part. Whittington, C. M. and colleagues, "Paternal nutrient provisioning during male pregnancy in the seahorse *Hippocampus abdominalis*," *J. Comp. Physiol. B* **190** (2020) — a **patrotrophy index of 1**, which is the measured answer to whether the father actually feeds the brood rather than merely housing pre-loaded yolk; Syngnathidae are the only vertebrates with male pregnancy, and the placenta-like pouch, circulatory connection and immune modulation are from the seahorse genomics literature, cited and not read in full. **The entry that matters most, because it corrects a textbook rather than an animal:** the common murre's pyriform egg is explained in book after book by rolling in a tight arc or spinning like a top, and **there is no good evidence for it** — Ingold (1980) found little support, and Birkhead, T. R. & Thompson, J. E. and colleagues reject it in "Egg shape in the Common Guillemot *Uria aalge* and Brünnich's Guillemot *U. lomvia*: not a rolling matter?", the decisive objection being that **the rolling arc is much wider than the breeding ledge**. The replacement carried here is Birkhead, T. R. and colleagues, "The pyriform egg of the Common Murre (*Uria aalge*) is more stable on sloping surfaces," *The Auk* **135**(4), 1020–1032 (2018), with the mechanical-strength and contamination accounts given as *hypotheses*. Read at abstract and summary level, not in full — and the card says on its face that this entry is about us rather than about a bird. **Held honestly, with figures withheld rather than dressed up:** the kiwi's 15–22% of female body mass is printed as a **range** assembled from record-keeping and reference sources (Guinness World Records, the Smithsonian, Save the Kiwi) rather than from a primary morphometric study, published values differ, and the page says it is the figure we would most like to improve; the popular *explanation* for the egg's size (a retained relic of larger ancestors) is a real hypothesis with real argument against it and is **deliberately not asserted**. The brine shrimp's ~18-year diapause is given as a **reported maximum**, not a measured record, with the 17-year Great Salt Lake series (Belovsky, *Limnology and Oceanography* **64**, 2019: hatchability 17.8–78.8%, overwinter survival 9.9–65.9%) carrying the real measurement; claims of far longer dormancy in other crustaceans are not carried. Megapode figures — buried 40–150 cm, median ~40 h to dig out (range 26–55), superprecocial with same-day flight in some species — are from the Journal of Ornithology work on brush-turkey chicks after underground hatching plus Eiby & Booth, *The Auk* **125**(3), 594 (2008), both at abstract level. Sea turtle TSD (pivotal ≈29 °C, some populations nearer 30.4 °C, ~100% female above roughly 31 °C, ~99% female bias reported for northern Great Barrier Reef green turtles) is compiled at review level, **with the local-adaptation counterweight printed in the same breath** rather than left out to sharpen the point. Sand tiger reproduction, mouthbrooding durations, monotreme physiology and *Pipa* oviposition are given as well-established natural history with precise figures withheld where sources are secondary. **One precision that is the kind lost first in retelling:** back-brooding is **Belostomatinae**, not Belostomatidae — *Lethocerus* guards eggs on emergent vegetation and does not carry them — and the card states the distinction rather than saying "giant water bugs carry eggs on their backs." **The borrowed word is credited as unowned, exactly as in No. 57:** *egg* / *getting your egg cracked* is trans community language with no established coiner; Bauer, E., *Them*, 5 December 2024, traces the earliest documented use to an Urban Dictionary *egg mode* entry of 23 August 2016 and records Susan Stryker unable to date her own first encounter. The Autistic/ADHD extension is **Essy Knopf**'s, who credits the trans origin himself. **The sixteenth card carries no reframe table, and this is the fifth distinct reason on the site for withholding the apparatus**, after FG 10 (a cosmology cannot take it), FG 12 (no verdict to correct), FG 14 (a verdict that is a wish rather than a misreading) and FG 15 (the reader's own room). Here: every table above corrects a mistaken verdict about an *animal*, and **nobody has reached a mistaken verdict about the morning somebody's own life rearranged itself behind them** — a column headed *actually* pointed at that would be an insult wearing the costume of rigour. **Deliberately out of scope:** the **Arrau turtle**, whose embryos call from inside the shell — already drawn in `hatchery.html` and carried in No. 57, so a third telling was declined; brood parasitism as a *nesting* strategy, which is FG 15's; and the emperor penguin's feet, likewise FG 15's. **The tenth guide to arrive independently at *there is no standard {X}*** — counted by grep across the guides, not from memory. **Two faults caught by eye that no gate could see, which is the standing lesson about SVG glyphs:** the guide shipped its murre egg as a near-symmetric oval when *the taper is the entire subject of the entry*, so the drawing was making a weaker claim than the prose — redrawn properly pyriform, with a source comment saying why; and a `<div class="starfield">` element was invented for the body that exists in no other field guide and that the CSS does not define, an inert empty div that was removed rather than styled (this guide's starfield is `body::before`, inherited with the sheet). **A pre-existing fault found while wiring the chain, and it is a fault class no gate covers:** `collection-sound.html`'s `ss-nav-prev` pointed at `nests-field-guide.html` while its `.ss-nav-label` read *The Ways of Being Alone* — a reader clicking it landed somewhere other than the label promised. Introduced when FG 15 was inserted on the 16th and invisible to everything: the chain check compares hrefs only, and `check-markup.mjs` validates the *collection badge* label but not prev/next labels. Fixed here. **A site-wide audit was then run rather than assuming it was the only one**, and it found no others: a first pass flagged 21 pages, all of them legitimate house abbreviations (*Wyrd & Weird*, *Garden & Stars*, *LYDTYSS*) or entity-encoding differences, which is a false-positive rate that would make a gate unreadable — refining the test to *does this label name a different page* returned only two hits, both false positives where "LYDTYSS" correctly abbreviates *Love You Down To Your Star Stuff* and merely also appears in `shorthand-evolution.html`'s title. **A second pre-existing fault, in the count the ledger itself settled yesterday:** the companion figure recorded as *six and eight* on 2026-08-17 was still a marker-derived undercount. Re-deriving it by grepping colophons instead of trusting `.card-series` found `monotropic-galaxy-field-guide.html` naming **two** companion zines (Nos. 45 and 46) with no marker at all, so the honest figures were *seven and seven* before this guide and are **eight and seven** after it. Its marker has been added and `CLAUDE.md` now records the real lesson: the fix for a marker-derived count was applied to the two pages that prompted it and never swept across the rest, so the corrected count inherited the original defect. **Counts re-derived rather than incremented**, per the section of `CLAUDE.md` with a measured failure rate: 15 guides, **182 anchored cards, 174 entries** (182 minus the eight cards no guide files as entries — the five turtles people made, the octopus settlements, the rooms people build, and now the egg-crack accounts, the five confirmed by counting the rendered DOM after the source's `kind: 'made'` grep gave six). The collection page was printing its stale figures in **eight** places — `meta`, `og:`, `twitter:`, JSON-LD, `.hero-count`, two body paragraphs and the colophon line — exactly as `CLAUDE.md` warns, and all eight were corrected. All six gates clean: markup (118 pages, 0 problems, 103/103 collection badges across 13 collections), sitemap (118 entries, 0 problems), chain (102 pages, no prev/next mismatches in either direction), contrast (0/799 screen, 0/772 print, 62 SVG labels measured, 2 gradient-clipped unmeasured), overlap (1,958 text boxes, 0 collisions, 0 clipped), and the search index rebuilt at 16 records / 100% coverage. **Open:** (1) the **kiwi 15–22% range** rests on reference and record-keeping sources, not a primary morphometric study; (2) **Birkhead's murre papers were read at abstract and summary level**, not in full, which matters more here than usual because this entry overturns a textbook claim; (3) **Vince-adjacent and megapode papers were read at abstract level**, as were the sea-turtle TSD sources, which are review-level rather than a single primary; (4) the **echidna egg-tooth/caruncle primary** (Hughes and colleagues, *Developmental Biology*, 2022) is cited and unread, as are the seahorse immunogenetics papers, the cardinalfish coloration and parentage papers, and Wyffels et al. (2022) on sand tiger reproduction; (5) the **cuckoo arms-race challenge is a 2025 preprint** that has not been peer reviewed — it is flagged on the card as a live challenge and nothing leans on it, and the 2025 *Science* paper on the genomic architecture of egg mimicry is cited unread; (6) **`tortoise-field-guide.html` is a companion to a *guide* rather than a zine** and is counted separately on the collection page's face rather than folded into the eight — a category judgement, not a measurement, and worth revisiting if a second guide-to-guide companion ever appears. |

| Manifesto | 2026-08-09 | CORRECTED → VERIFIED | First content check, including the nine runs of text that had been *invisible* until the 2026-08-08 render fix (the inline block re-declared nine `--sp-*` tokens as `--sp-x: var(--sp-x)`, a cycle resolving to nothing; accents inherited white and the gradient headings kept `-webkit-text-fill-color: transparent`, blanking nine passages including the page's own `<h1>`). **Five corrections.** (1) **Bone piezoelectricity was attributed to the wrong material, twice.** The page said hydroxyapatite is the piezoelectric component ("Hydroxyapatite… is one of them"; "Hydroxyapatite crystals in compact bone convert compression to charge"). The effect Fukada & Yasuda measured is **collagen's** — its fibres are arranged without a centre of symmetry and generate charge when sheared past one another; hydroxyapatite is centrosymmetric, which rules out classical piezoelectricity, and its role in bone's electrical environment is a recent and secondary finding. Both passages rewritten, with the hydroxyapatite caveat kept rather than deleted. (2) **Eiichi Fukada restored to the credit.** The page had "Japanese orthopedic surgeon Dr. Iwao Yasuda discovered"; the physicist co-author of *On the Piezoelectric Effect of Bone* (*J. Phys. Soc. Japan* 12:1158–1162, 1957) was missing — the pairing is already correct in No. 1's row. (3) **Nucleosynthesis overclaim**, the same class of error corrected in No. 2: "Every atom heavier than hydrogen and helium was manufactured inside a star" is false for lithium, beryllium and boron — most ⁷Li is primordial, Be and B come almost entirely from cosmic-ray spallation in the interstellar medium, and stars *destroy* all three. Now "almost every," with the three exceptions named. (4) **"Hypergiants a thousand times the mass of our sun"** conflated mass with radius: red hypergiants like UY Scuti run ~7–10 M☉ at ~900+ solar *radii*, while the most massive star known, R136a1, is ~196 M☉ (169–230; Gemini South/Zorro, 2022) against a theoretical ceiling near 300. Rewritten to give radius and mass separately. (5) **A near-verbatim Sagan passage presented as speech but altered** — "Carl Sagan said it plainly: the nitrogen in our DNA, the calcium in our teeth, the iron in our blood — forged in the cores of stars that no longer exist." This is the exact pattern corrected in No. 1. Now quoted verbatim and cited: "The nitrogen in our DNA, the calcium in our teeth, the iron in our blood, the carbon in our apple pies were made in the interiors of collapsing stars" (*Cosmos*, 1980, ch. 9). **Verified, unchanged:** Greek *piezein* "to press"; bone remodelling under load; the neurodiversity paradigm's variation-not-deficit claim; the social model applied to nervous systems; the DSM as a historically situated document. The eight "We hold" declarations are statements of value, not factual claims, and are ours. ~~**Open:** the page carries **no colophon or source block** — alone among the collection's argument pieces.~~ **Closed 2026-08-09 — colophon added** at Ryan's direction, built from this pass's citations and using the page's own `sp-` type scale. Six blocks: what the piece is; §I origin (B²FH 1957; Kobayashi, Karakas & Lugaro 2020 for the per-site accounting; the Li/Be/B exception; Sagan *Cosmos* 1980 ch. 9); §II & VI the bones (Fukada & Yasuda 1957 with the collagen mechanism stated and the hydroxyapatite caveat kept; Bassett; Wolff's law); §III the paradigm (Walker for both paradigm terms; Botha et al. 2024 for the collective origin; Blume 1997/98; InLv/Dekker 1996; Sinclair and ANI; Asasumasu 2000; the social model; red-dwarf longevity and the R136a1 mass figure); a **Held honestly** note; and a **Corrections** block naming all five of this pass's fixes on the page itself, including that some of the corrected text had been invisible until the day before. The Held-honestly note is the load-bearing one: it states plainly that the science does not make the ethics true, that deriving an ought from an is is a fallacy we would rather name than commit, and that bone piezoelectricity is an *analogy* for responsive nervous systems rather than evidence about them. Render and text-visibility re-verified after editing (all six changed passages visible, `-webkit-text-fill-color` matching `color`, non-zero heights). |
| Inclusion Safety — A Star Stuff Creed | 2026-08-11 | CORRECTED → VERIFIED | First check, triggered by building the broadside companion — the creed's quotations are load-bearing on a sheet meant to be pinned at a door, so they were traced before being set in type. **Timothy R. Clark**, *The 4 Stages of Psychological Safety: Defining the Path to Inclusion and Innovation* (Berrett-Koehler, 2020): all four quoted lines confirmed **verbatim in the book's Stage 1**, against a photographed page of the primary supplied by Ryan Boren — "Inclusion safety is not earned but owed," "Giving inclusion safety is a moral imperative," "If you have flesh and blood, we accept you," and "Including another human being should be an act of prejudgment based on that person's worth, not an act of judgment based on that person's worthiness." Note for anyone re-checking: an intermediate pass could **not** find the moral-imperative sentence in the licensed book text it could reach, and provisionally treated it as a compression — a wrong call, corrected by the primary. The book's own sentence following "not earned but owed" is "Every human has title to it as a nonnegotiable right," which is a *different* sentence, and that is the real finding: **the creed's pull-quote splices two non-adjacent sentences from Stage 1 without marking the gap.** An ellipsis was added, and the attribution now names the edition and the stage. *Be human, be harmless* is a faithful compression of Clark's qualification, not a quotation, and is set unquoted; confirmed against LeaderFactor (Clark's own company), which states you qualify "simply by being human and harmless." Four stages confirmed in order: inclusion, learner, contributor, challenger. **Douglass citation added, and dated.** The creed attributed "I know of no rights of race superior to the rights of humanity" to **Frederick Douglass** with no work and no date. It is from ***Our Composite Nationality*, Boston, 7 December 1869**, per the scholarly Frederick Douglass Papers Project edition. Recorded because it is a live trap for a future editor: the Library of Congress catalogues its manuscript of the lecture as **1867**, and at least one reputable teaching repository repeats that date, so the two circulate together. The quoted clause is stable across every source consulted; the sentence continues "…and when there is a supposed conflict between human and national rights, it is safe to go to the side of humanity," and only the first clause is set. The move past common humanity to common **origin** — belonging as constitutive because we are made of the same star stuff — is ours and is marked as ours. |
| The Star Stuff You're Made Of (broadside) | 2026-08-12 | VERIFIED — carries a correction to its own parent | Two-sided print sheet, companion to **Field Guide No. 2**. Abundances, house names and forge sites all inherit that guide's row above, **including the iron correction this build produced** — see that row. **The sheet is the reason the error was found:** drawing a one-line forge label for each element forced every origin to be stated in its shortest form, and "supernovae" for iron would not survive being set next to "Type Ia" on the verso. Compression as a checking method, which is worth noting because it is the opposite of the usual worry about compression. Iron is therefore given as *two kinds of stellar death, roughly half each*, on both sides, and the verso's Type Ia panel says plainly that this is "the half of the story that usually gets left out." **Hedges kept:** percentages marked approximate and by mass; the r-process attribution stated as resting on the 2017 neutron-star merger and as current best evidence rather than a closed question; the Hoyle resonance told as Hoyle reasoning backward from the existence of carbon-based observers, without implying design — the guide's own careful framing ("we won't claim intent"). **Element order is the guide's** (H, O, C, N, Ca, Fe, K, I) — narrative, not by abundance or atomic number — and the stylesheet header records that re-sorting it would be an editorial change to the parent dressed as a layout decision. **The refusals are carried from the collection's standing set, not invented for this sheet** — the not-a-distinction, rhyme-not-proof and not-ownership refusals restate positions already held across the collection and gathered on `what-we-refuse-broadside.html`; Field Guide No. 2 has no "Not:" block of its own, so this is the one place the sheet adds a house convention its parent lacks, and it adds no new *claim*. Sources: Burbidge, Burbidge, Fowler & Hoyle (1957) for stellar nucleosynthesis; Kobayashi, Karakas & Lugaro (2020) for the iron split. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 2 printed sides on **both** A4 and US Letter, and on the first measurement rather than after trimming — the 4×2 element grid is a more predictable block than the prose versos that failed twice each; 346 tags, markup clean; contrast clear on screen and in print. |
| Not a Line (broadside) | 2026-08-15 | VERIFIED (derivative) — one open item; **consent question closed, author approval received** | Two-sided print sheet, the functioning-labels field card, companion to **No. 21**, whose row above is `VERIFIED`. **It introduces no claim that zine does not carry**, which was checked line by line rather than assumed: the spectroscopy (dispersion, Fraunhofer lines, Kirchhoff & Bunsen, each element's fingerprint), the severity-scale critique, the spiky profile, Patrick Dwyer's *autism constellation*, Helen Edgar's *constellation of intensities*, and the guardrail that refusing the line is not refusing that some of us need far more support. The five "what it actually does" rows on the face are compressions of No. 21's spreads 3 and 6 — *"mild to severe" measures how much you inconvenience us*, and functioning labels as gatekeeping in both directions (*not disabled enough* / *too disabled to be trusted with our own choices*) — not new arguments. **Attribution carried exact, and deliberately more explicit than the parent in one place:** No. 21's spread 4 describes the spiky profile without naming anyone, so the face credits **Damian Milton, building on Francesca Happé**, taking the attribution from this ledger's own No. 8 row rather than from memory. **The community lineage of the constellation metaphor is on the sheet, not just in the zine** — Dwyer's 2021 note that he did not coin it, Caroline Hearst's physical model, Milton crediting **Larry Arnold** as the first he knows of to use *constellation* in place of *spectrum*, and Dinah Murray — because a sheet arguing against ranking people should not erase the people whose idea it is, and a broadside travels further than the page holding its footnotes. Helen Edgar's quoted line ("always in motion, always connecting, always becoming — never fixed, never finished") is verbatim from No. 21, where it is logged as her own words. *Monotropism* → Murray, Lesser & Lawson (2005). **THE OPEN CONSENT QUESTION, and it is the reason this sheet did not exist until today.** `collection-print.html` carried a public note holding this card back: No. 21 is after Helen Edgar, *constellation of intensities* is her phrase, and **a broadside circulates harder than a zine** — walls, meetings, photocopiers — so moving her phrase into that circulation was recorded as a decision to ask about rather than assume. **Closed the same day — Helen Edgar reviewed a PDF of the built sheet and approved it** (relayed by Ryan Boren, 2026-08-15). That is the sequence the hold was for: the sheet was built, shown to her as the artifact it would actually be rather than described, and shipped on her word. It is the same procedure as No. 30's author sign-off. The collection note was rewritten to record the hold and its lifting rather than deleted, on the grounds that a hold which vanishes on the day it lifts teaches nobody anything. **One departure from house convention, stated on the sheet's own stylesheet header:** it carries **no "Not:" refusals block**, alone in the rack. The face already is a refusals list — five phrases, each with the thing it does — so a second block restated the front of the sheet in smaller type; it was cut when side B ran 234px over, and cutting it was the right editorial call independently of the fit. **One design decision that is an argument, also in the header:** the spiky profile is drawn on the face *complete with grid and baseline*, even though the sheet's position is that a better chart is still a chart. That is deliberate — nearly everyone reaching for this card has met the spiky profile and thinks it is the answer, and you cannot refuse a picture you have not shown. The grid is dropped on the verso, so the face→verso sequence **is** the argument. **Render checks (measured, not assumed):** 2 printed sides on **both** A4 and US Letter, reached after two failed measurements (side B 234px over, then 42px, then 13px) and trimmed until clean; 59 screen and 100 print text elements including 16 SVG labels, zero AA failures in either medium; 98 text boxes, 0 collisions, 0 clipped; markup clean, badge and collection card added in the same pass, chain verified in both directions. **The figure was inspected by eye at render size and was wrong on the first draft** — the "expected baseline" label sat inside the plot and ran straight through the profile line between *social* and *planning*. No gate here catches that: `check-overlap.mjs` measures text against text and says outright that text crossing artwork is outside what it reports. The plot was shortened to x=404 and the label moved into its own right margin. **Open:** the Ollibean / ASAN / "Words That Wound" functioning-labels citations are inherited from No. 21's colophon at the level that zine states them, and were not independently re-read at their primaries this pass. |
